Condensed Mathematics — Condensed Sets, Solid Modules & the Analytic Patching

Condensed mathematics is a new foundation for analytic geometry developed by Dustin Clausen and Peter Scholze. It replaces topological spaces with condensed sets — sheaves on the site of profinite sets — creating a "convenient category" that contains all topological spaces while allowing free constructions, quotients, and internal Hom objects that fail in the topological category. The site ProFin consists of profinite sets (totally disconnected compact Hausdorff spaces) with finite families of jointly surjective maps as coverings. A condensed set is a sheaf on this site. Every topological space T determines a condensed set via S ↦ Cont(S, T) (continuous maps from a profinite set S to T). Unlike topological spaces, the category Cond is a topos — it is cartesian closed, has all limits and colimits, and admits a well-behaved notion of abelian group objects. From this foundation, Clausen and Scholze build solid modules (the condensed analog of complete topological vector spaces) and liquid modules (an analytic generalization over ℝ), reconstructing large parts of functional analysis without the usual pathologies (non-closed sums of subspaces, failures of the open mapping theorem, etc.). The theory culminates in the Fargues-Scholze geometric local Langlands correspondence for GL_n over ℚ_p, a landmark proof using the Fargues-Fontaine curve and the theory of shtukas, where condensed mathematics provides the language for the stack of G-bundles as a small v-stack.

"Condensed mathematics is a new foundation for analytic geometry."
— Peter Scholze
Part 1

📏 Condensed Sets

Foundations
ProFin, Sheaves, and the Topos Cond
The site of profinite sets ProFin has objects profinite sets (cofiltered limits of finite sets, equivalently totally disconnected compact Hausdorff spaces) and coverings given by finite families of surjections {S_i → S} that are jointly surjective. A condensed set X is a sheaf on ProFin: a functor X: ProFin^op → Set such that for any surjection S' → S of profinite sets, the diagram X(S) → X(S') ⇉ X(S' ×_S S') is an equalizer. Equivalently, condensed sets are pyknotic sets (Barwick-Haine) — the difference is a set-theoretic size condition. Every topological space T gives a condensed set via T(S) = Cont(S, T), the set of continuous maps from S to T. This functor is fully faithful when T is compactly generated Hausdorff, so condensed sets generalize such spaces. The category Cond is a topos — it has finite limits, is cartesian closed (admits internal Hom objects), and has a subobject classifier. This solves classical problems: in Top, the quotient of a topological space by an equivalence relation does not always have the correct universal property; in Cond, quotients and colimits are always well-behaved. The internal Hom Hom_Cond(X, Y) is a condensed set defined by Hom_Cond(X, Y)(S) = Hom_Cond(X × S, Y). The existence of the internal Hom allows the construction of mapping spaces, loop spaces, and free condensed abelian groups that are impossible in the ordinary topological setting.
Condensed sets replace topological spaces with sheaves on profinite sets, yielding a topos where quotients, internal Homs, and free constructions exist — resolving the foundational pathologies of general topology.
Part 2

🌍 Condensed Abelian Groups and Solid Modules

Solid
Condensed Abelian Groups, Solidification, and the Derived Category
A condensed abelian group is a sheaf of abelian groups on ProFin — equivalently, an abelian group object in Cond. The category Cond(Ab) is a Grothendieck abelian category with enough projectives. Its derived category D(Cond(Ab)) is well-behaved. A condensed abelian group M is called solid if for every profinite set S = lim← S_i (written as a cofiltered limit of finite sets), the natural map (ℤ[S]^∧) ⊗ M → M(S) is an isomorphism, where ℤ[S]^∧ = lim← ℤ[S_i] is the completed free condensed abelian group on S. Equivalently, M is solid iff for every profinite set S, the map from the free condensed abelian group ℤ[S]^∧ to M is an isomorphism. The solidification functor M ↦ M^■ is the left adjoint to the inclusion Solid(Ab) → Cond(Ab). Solid modules form a full abelian subcategory closed under limits, colimits, and extensions. The key theorem (Scholze): the derived category D(Solid) is equivalent to the derived category of ℤ-vector spaces — D(Solid) ≅ D(ℤ). This is remarkable: it says that the homological algebra of solid modules reduces to ordinary homological algebra of ℤ-modules. Solid modules are the "correct" category for analytic geometry in the p-adic world, analogous to how complete topological vector spaces behave well in functional analysis. The condensed version of Banach spaces and the condensed approach to locally convex vector spaces eliminates the classic pathologies: the sum of two closed subspaces is always closed in the condensed sense, and the open mapping theorem holds universally.
Solid modules are the condensed analogue of complete topological vector spaces; their derived category reduces to D(ℤ), making them the ideal setting for p-adic analytic geometry.
Part 3

🧮 Analytic Rings and Liquid Modules

Analytic
Analytic Rings, Liquid Modules, and the Reconstruction of Functional Analysis
An analytic ring (A, A_■) is a condensed ring A together with a solid A-module structure A_■ — the "completion" functor — satisfying axioms that generalize the solid/liquid dichotomy. For the real numbers ℝ, the liquid module theory provides a category of "complete locally convex topological vector spaces" over ℝ equipped with the liquid condition: for each real number p ≥ 1, the liquid ℝ-module ℝ_{L^p spaces, Sobolev spaces, Schwartz distributions, and the theory of nuclear spaces all arise naturally as solid or liquid modules. The pathologies of topological vector spaces disappear: in the condensed/liquid framework, the sum of two closed subspaces is always closed, the open mapping theorem holds for all liquid Banach spaces, and the projective and injective tensor products are well-behaved. The condensed Hochschild homology and condensed cyclic homology define a cohomology theory for analytic rings that specializes to classical de Rham cohomology for smooth algebras and to p-adic Hodge theory for perfectoid rings. The analytic rings thus provide a unified framework for archimedean and non-archimedean geometry, bridging the gap between complex analysis and p-adic analysis.
Liquid modules over ℝ provide a pathology-free foundation for functional analysis, unifying archimedean and non-archimedean geometry through the concept of analytic rings.
Part 4

🔥 Applications: Fargues-Fontaine Curve and the Local Langlands

Langlands
The Fargues-Fontaine Curve, G-Bundles, and the Geometric Local Langlands
The Fargues-Fontaine curve X is a complete algebraic curve (over ℚ_p) that classifies untilts of a perfectoid field. Its remarkable property: the category of quasi-coherent sheaves on X is equivalent to the category of (φ, Γ)-modules over the Robba ring, realizing p-adic Galois representations geometrically. The Fargues-Scholze geometric Langlands correspondence (2021-2024) is a geometric version of the local Langlands program for GL_n(ℚ_p) using the Fargues-Fontaine curve and shtukas. Clausen and Scholze use condensed mathematics to define the stack of G-bundles Bun_G on the Fargues-Fontaine curve as a "condensed stack" — a small v-stack. The key theorem: the category of Weil representations of the Weil group of ℚ_p is equivalent to the category of certain perverse sheaves (with ℚ̅_ℓ-coefficients) on Bun_G. The derived Satake equivalence for the Fargues-Fontaine curve in the condensed setting provides the geometric input: the category of equivariant perverse sheaves on the affine Grassmannian is equivalent to the category of representations of the Langlands dual group. The Fargues-Scholze theorem constructs the local Langlands correspondence for GL_n(ℚ_p) purely geometrically, recovering and reinterpreting the classical local Langlands correspondence (Harris-Taylor, Henniart) through the geometry of the Fargues-Fontaine curve and the condensed formalism. This application demonstrates the power of condensed mathematics: it provides the necessary language to treat infinite-dimensional moduli spaces (like Bun_G) as algebraic geometric objects while retaining the analytic completeness properties essential for p-adic geometry.
The Fargues-Scholze geometric local Langlands correspondence uses condensed mathematics to give a purely geometric proof of the local Langlands correspondence for GL_n, via the Fargues-Fontaine curve and the stack of G-bundles as a condensed v-stack.

🎯 Practice

← Back to Math Map

The Six-Functor Formalism in Condensed Étale Cohomology

The condensed framework extends the classical six-functor formalism of étale cohomology to the setting of diamonds and v‑stacks. In Scholze’s theory of diamonds, the étale site of a diamond is replaced by the v‑site, and the category of condensed abelian groups provides the natural coefficients. The functors f*, f*, f!, f!, ⊗, and Hom are defined on the derived category of condensed abelian groups, and they satisfy the expected adjunctions and base‑change formulas. A key input is the fact that the category of condensed sets is an ∞‑topos, which guarantees the existence of all limits and colimits needed for the construction of the functors. The proper base‑change theorem holds for v‑proper maps, and the smooth base‑change theorem holds for v‑smooth maps, mirroring the classical statements for schemes. The formalism also incorporates the projection formula and the Verdier duality functor, which in the condensed setting takes the form of a dualizing complex that is compactly generated. These results are proved by reducing to the case of perfectoid spaces, where the v‑topology coincides with the pro‑étale topology, and then using the solidity of the coefficient modules to pass to limits. The six‑functor formalism in condensed étale cohomology is the engine behind the construction of the excursion operators in the Fargues–Scholze geometric Langlands correspondence.

Key Properties

  • The derived category Dcond(X, Λ) of condensed Λ‑modules on a diamond X is compactly generated by objects of the form f!Λ for v‑proper maps f.
  • For a v‑proper map f, the functor f! coincides with f*, and the proper base‑change isomorphism f*g* ≅ g′*f′* holds in the condensed derived category.
  • The dualizing complex ωX is a solid object, and Verdier duality is given by RHom(−, ωX).

The Solid t‑Structure and the Derived Category of Solid Modules

The category of solid modules over a condensed ring A carries a natural t‑structure, called the solid t‑structure, whose heart is the abelian category of solid A‑modules. This t‑structure is left‑complete and right‑complete, and the truncation functors τ≤n and τ≥n are computed by applying the solidification functor to the usual truncations in the derived category of condensed A‑modules. The fundamental theorem of Clausen–Scholze states that for A = ℤ the derived category D(Solidℤ) is equivalent to the ordinary derived category D(ℤ) of abelian groups. The equivalence is realized by the forgetful functor from solid ℤ‑modules to condensed ℤ‑modules, which is fully faithful and admits a left adjoint given by solidification. As a consequence, the solid t‑structure on D(Solidℤ) corresponds under this equivalence to the standard t‑structure on D(ℤ). For a general condensed ring A, the solid t‑structure is compatible with the forgetful functor to condensed A‑modules, and the heart consists precisely of those condensed A‑modules that satisfy the solidity condition: for every extremally disconnected profinite set S, the natural map M(S) → HomCond(ℤ[S], M) is an isomorphism. This characterization allows one to test solidity on a set of generators, which is crucial for applications to p‑adic Hodge theory where one works with solid Banach spaces over ℚp.

Consequences for Homological Algebra

  • Ext groups between solid modules are computed by the usual derived functors in D(ℤ) after forgetting the solid structure.
  • The solidification functor is exact and preserves compact objects, so compact solid modules correspond to finitely generated abelian groups under the equivalence.
  • The derived tensor product of solid modules is again solid, and the internal Hom of solid modules is solid, making D(SolidA) a closed symmetric monoidal stable ∞‑category.
Worked Example: Verifying Solidity of a Condensed ℤ‑Module
Let M be the condensed ℤ‑module represented by the profinite set ℤp with its natural topology. Show that M is solid.
To verify solidity we must check that for every extremally disconnected profinite set S the map M(S) → HomCond(ℤ[S], M) is an isomorphism. Since M is represented by ℤp, M(S) = Cont(S, ℤp) the group of continuous maps. On the other hand, HomCond(ℤ[S], M) = HomCond(ℤ[S], ℤp) = Cont(S, ℤp) by the Yoneda lemma for condensed sets. The map is the identity, hence an isomorphism. Therefore M is solid.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks to prove that a condensed module is solid, reduce to the definition: test the isomorphism on a set of generators of the category of extremally disconnected profinite sets, typically the Stone–Čech compactifications of discrete sets. Use the fact that representable condensed modules are automatically solid, and that solidity is preserved under limits, colimits, and extensions.

Prismatic Cohomology and Condensed p‑adic Hodge Theory

Prismatic cohomology, developed by Bhatt and Scholze, provides a universal cohomology theory for p‑adic formal schemes that specializes to crystalline, de Rham, and étale cohomology. In the condensed setting, the prismatic site is replaced by the v‑site of a perfectoid space, and the prismatic complex is realized as a solid object in the derived category of solid ℤp‑modules. The key theorem states that for a smooth formal scheme 𝔛 over 𝒪C (C a perfectoid field) there is a natural isomorphism between the prismatic cohomology Δ𝔛 and the condensed étale cohomology RΓv(𝔛η, ℤp) after applying the solidification functor. This identification uses the fact that the prismatic complex is a solid ℤp‑module and that the v‑topology on the generic fiber 𝔛η is subcanonical for solid coefficients. The condensed approach also yields a clean proof of the Hodge–Tate comparison: the graded pieces of the Nygaard filtration on Δ𝔛 are identified with the condensed Hodge–Tate cohomology groups, which are solid Banach spaces over C. Moreover, the Breuil–Kisin–Fargues modules that classify p‑divisible groups over 𝒪C are naturally objects of the category of solid ℤp‑modules equipped with a Frobenius semi‑linear endomorphism. The equivalence of categories between p‑divisible groups and Breuil–Kisin–Fargues modules is then a statement entirely within the abelian category of solid modules, avoiding the analytic technicalities of the classical theory.

Applications to the de Rham Comparison

  • The condensed de Rham complex of a smooth rigid space is a solid object whose cohomology computes the condensed de Rham cohomology.
  • The filtered φ‑module structure on prismatic cohomology translates into a filtration on the solid de Rham complex, giving a condensed version of the Fontaine functor DdR.
  • All comparison isomorphisms become isomorphisms in D(Solidℤp), hence they are compatible with all derived functors.

Condensed Stacks and the Geometric Langlands Program for General Reductive Groups

The Fargues–Scholze construction of the stack BunG of G‑bundles on the Fargues–Fontaine curve uses the language of v‑stacks, which are sheaves of ∞‑groupoids on the category of perfectoid spaces equipped with the v‑topology. In the condensed formalism, BunG is a small v‑stack, meaning that its functor of points takes values in condensed sets and satisfies v‑descent. The geometric Satake equivalence in this setting identifies the category of equivariant perverse sheaves on the affine Grassmannian GrG with the category of representations of the Langlands dual group LG, both categories being realized as full subcategories of the derived category of solid ℚℓ‑modules on the relevant v‑stacks. The Hecke operators act on the category of sheaves on BunG via correspondences that are proper and smooth in the v‑topology, and the six‑functor formalism guarantees that these operators preserve the subcategory of perverse sheaves. The excursion algebra, generated by the Hecke operators and the Frobenius, is then a commutative algebra object in the condensed derived category, and its spectrum parametrizes the Langlands parameters. The main theorem of Fargues–Scholze (2021) constructs a bijection between the set of irreducible smooth representations of G(ℚp) and the set of isomorphism classes of certain perverse sheaves on BunG, which is functorial in G and compatible with the local Langlands correspondence for GLn. The condensed framework is essential because it allows one to treat the infinite‑dimensional moduli space BunG as a geometric object while retaining the analytic completeness needed for p‑adic coefficients.

Key Technical Points

  • BunG is a small v‑stack, hence a condensed stack; its diagonal is representable by a v‑sheaf.
  • The category of solid ℚℓ‑modules on BunG admits a t‑structure whose heart is the category of perverse sheaves.
  • The Hecke functors are defined using the proper pushforward and smooth pullback in the six‑functor formalism for condensed étale cohomology.
  • The excursion algebra is a solid commutative algebra, and its spectrum is a condensed scheme that classifies Langlands parameters.
  • Assuming that the equivalence D(Solid) ≅ D(ℤ) holds for all condensed rings, not only for ℤ.
  • Confusing the solid t‑structure with the standard t‑structure on condensed modules; they differ for general rings.
  • Thinking that every condensed module is solid; solidity is a strong completeness condition.
  • Neglecting the role of extremally disconnected profinite sets as generators when testing solidity.
  • Believing that the six‑functor formalism for condensed étale cohomology works without the v‑topology; the v‑site is essential.
  • Overlooking that the prismatic comparison isomorphisms live in the derived category of solid modules, not in the classical derived category.
  • Misidentifying the stack BunG as an ordinary algebraic stack; it is a v‑stack and only becomes geometric after condensation.

The advanced topics above illustrate how condensed mathematics unifies homological algebra, p‑adic Hodge theory, and the geometric Langlands program within a single coherent framework.

The Pro-étale Site and Condensed Sheaves

The pro-étale site of a scheme or adic space was introduced by Bhatt and Scholze to provide a topology fine enough to capture perfectoid covers while remaining subcanonical for many sheaves of interest. In the condensed formalism, a sheaf on the pro-étale site takes values in condensed sets rather than ordinary sets. This means that for each affinoid perfectoid U = Spa(R,R⁺) the value F(U) is a condensed set, i.e. a sheaf on the site of extremally disconnected profinite sets. The assignment U ↦ F(U) satisfies descent for pro-étale covers, and the resulting category of condensed sheaves is an ∞‑topos. The key advantage is that limits and colimits in this ∞‑topos are computed objectwise on the level of condensed sets, which are themselves limits of profinite sets. Consequently, the pro-étale cohomology of a space with coefficients in a condensed abelian group can be computed by the derived global sections functor in the condensed derived category. This approach avoids the technicalities of working with ind‑pro‑objects in the classical étale topos and makes the six‑functor formalism available for coefficients that are not discrete. In particular, the v‑topology on perfectoid spaces is subcanonical for solid coefficients, so the v‑sheaf associated to a solid module is already a condensed sheaf on the pro-étale site. The comparison between pro-étale and v‑cohomology for solid coefficients is then a formal consequence of the fact that the v‑topology is generated by covers that are also pro-étale.

Condensed sheaves on the pro-étale site also provide a natural home for the completed structure sheaf 𝒪̂ and its derived variants. The sheaf 𝒪̂ takes values in condensed rings, and its derived global sections compute the prismatic cohomology of the space. Because the pro-étale site is defined using weakly étale maps, the cohomology groups are automatically solid, and the Nygaard filtration on the prismatic complex becomes a filtration by solid submodules. This perspective clarifies why the Hodge–Tate comparison is an isomorphism in the derived category of solid modules: both sides are computed as derived global sections of condensed sheaves on the same site, and the comparison map is induced by a map of condensed sheaves that is a quasi‑isomorphism on stalks. The pro-étale site therefore serves as the geometric backbone that makes the condensed six‑functor formalism work for p‑adic Hodge theory.

Another important feature is that the category of condensed sheaves on the pro-étale site is compactly generated by representable sheaves of the form h_U for affinoid perfectoid U. This compact generation allows one to apply Brown representability and to construct adjoint functors between categories of condensed sheaves on different sites. For example, the pushforward along a morphism of perfectoid spaces is computed by the right Kan extension of the corresponding condensed sheaves, and its left adjoint (the pullback) preserves compact objects. These properties are essential for defining the Hecke operators on the stack Bun_G as correspondences of condensed stacks, because the proper pushforward and smooth pullback in the six‑functor formalism are then guaranteed to exist and to preserve the subcategory of perverse sheaves.

Finally, the pro-étale site interacts well with the theory of diamonds. A diamond is a sheaf on the category of perfectoid spaces for the v‑topology that is locally representable. When viewed as a condensed sheaf on the pro-étale site, a diamond becomes a small v‑stack, and its functor of points takes values in condensed sets. This identification is used in the Fargues–Scholze construction of the local Langlands correspondence, where the stack Bun_G of G‑bundles on the Fargues–Fontaine curve is a diamond and hence a condensed stack. The pro-étale site thus provides a unified framework in which diamonds, condensed sheaves, and solid modules coexist and interact through the six‑functor formalism.

Derived Analytic Geometry and Condensed Rings

Derived analytic geometry, as developed by Porta and Yu, replaces the classical category of analytic spaces by an ∞‑category of derived analytic spaces whose structure sheaves take values in animated (i.e. simplicial) condensed rings. A condensed ring is a sheaf of rings on the site of extremally disconnected profinite sets; equivalently, it is a monoid object in the category of condensed sets. The analytic rings of Clausen and Scholze are a special class of condensed rings that encode the functional‑analytic structure of non‑archimedean fields: an analytic ring is a condensed ring A equipped with a compatible family of seminorms on the sets A(S) for extremally disconnected S. The category of modules over an analytic ring is the category of condensed modules that are complete with respect to the induced topology; this category is precisely the category of solid modules when the analytic ring is the analytic ring associated to a non‑archimedean field.

In derived analytic geometry, the structure sheaf 𝒪_X of a derived analytic space X is a sheaf of animated analytic rings. Its homotopy groups are ordinary analytic rings, and the underlying condensed ring of π₀𝒪_X recovers the classical analytic structure sheaf. The advantage of working with animated analytic rings is that derived tensor products and derived mapping spaces are computed internally in the category of condensed modules, where they are well behaved because the category of solid modules is a stable ∞‑category with a symmetric monoidal structure. This allows one to define the cotangent complex of a morphism of derived analytic spaces as an object in the derived category of solid modules, and to prove a derived version of the Grauert–Remmert theorem stating that the pushforward of a coherent solid module along a proper morphism is again coherent.

The connection with condensed mathematics is made explicit by the fact that the ∞‑category of solid modules over an analytic ring A is equivalent to the ∞‑category of modules over the corresponding condensed ring in the sense of Clausen–Scholze. Under this equivalence, the solid t‑structure on D(Solid_A) corresponds to the t‑structure whose heart is the category of discrete solid A‑modules. The derived analytic geometry of Porta–Yu therefore provides a geometric interpretation of the algebraic structures that appear in condensed mathematics: analytic rings are the condensed rings that arise from functional analysis, and solid modules are the modules that satisfy the completeness condition required for derived analytic geometry. This equivalence has been used to prove a condensed version of the Kiehl–Verdier theorem: the derived category of solid modules on a smooth rigid space is generated by the structure sheaf and its twists, and the six‑functor formalism for condensed étale cohomology is compatible with the derived analytic structure.

A concrete application is the construction of the prismatic complex in derived analytic geometry. The prismatic site of a p‑adic formal scheme can be realized as a derived analytic space over the analytic ring 𝔸_inf. The prismatic cohomology complex is then the derived global sections of the structure sheaf on this derived analytic space, and its Nygaard filtration is induced by the Hodge filtration on the derived de Rham complex. Because all objects live in the category of solid modules, the comparison isomorphisms with étale and de Rham cohomology become isomorphisms in D(Solid_ℤ_p). This approach avoids the use of almost mathematics and replaces it by the intrinsic completeness of solid modules, which is a more natural condition in the condensed setting.

Worked Example: Solidity of the Completed Structure Sheaf
Let X = Spa(C,𝒪_C) be a point with C an algebraically closed non‑archimedean field. Show that the condensed module 𝒪̂(X) of completed global sections is a solid ℤ_p‑module.
The completed structure sheaf on X is the condensed ring 𝒪̂(X) = C equipped with its natural topology. As a condensed set, C is the sheaf S ↦ Cont(S,C) of continuous maps from an extremally disconnected profinite set S to C. For any extremally disconnected S, the set Cont(S,C) is a compact Hausdorff topological ℤ_p‑module because C is a complete non‑archimedean field and S is profinite. The functor S ↦ Cont(S,C) sends finite disjoint unions to products, and it satisfies the sheaf condition for the canonical topology on extremally disconnected sets. By definition, a condensed ℤ_p‑module M is solid if for every extremally disconnected S the natural map M(S) → HomCond(ℤ_p)(ℤ_p[S], M) is an isomorphism. For M = C, the left side is Cont(S,C). The right side is the set of condensed ℤ_p‑linear maps from the free condensed module on S to C. Such a map is uniquely determined by its values on the generators S, which must be a continuous map S → C because the condensed module structure on C is given by continuous maps. Hence the map is a bijection, and C is solid. This argument generalises to any smooth rigid space: the completed structure sheaf is a sheaf of solid modules because its values on affinoid perfectoids are solid Banach spaces, and solidity is a local property for the v‑topology.
Exam Strategy: Recognising Solidity in Concrete Settings
When a question asks whether a given condensed module is solid, first identify the underlying condensed set. If it is of the form S ↦ Cont(S,V) for a complete topological vector space V over a non‑archimedean field, then it is solid. The key test is whether the module satisfies the universal property with respect to free condensed modules on extremally disconnected sets. In practice, solid modules are exactly those that are derived complete for the topology generated by the seminorms coming from the analytic ring. Remember that not every condensed module is solid; discreteness alone does not imply solidity unless the module is also compactly generated.

The Role of Extremally Disconnected Sets in Solidity

Extremally disconnected profinite sets are the projective generators of the category of condensed sets. A profinite set S is extremally disconnected if the closure of every open subset is open; equivalently, the Boolean algebra of clopen subsets of S is complete. The fundamental theorem of Clausen and Scholze states that the family of representable functors h_S = HomProFin(-,S) for extremally disconnected S forms a set of compact projective generators of the ∞‑topos of condensed sets. This means that any condensed set X can be recovered as the colimit of the diagram of representables mapping to X, and that mapping out of h_S commutes with all colimits. In the category of condensed abelian groups, the free condensed abelian group ℤ[S] on an extremally disconnected S is a compact projective generator. Consequently, a condensed abelian group M is determined by its values M(S) = Hom(ℤ[S], M) on extremally disconnected S, and a morphism of condensed abelian groups is an isomorphism if and only if it induces isomorphisms on all M(S).

The definition of solidity is formulated entirely in terms of these generators. A condensed ℤ_p‑module M is solid if for every extremally disconnected S the natural map M(S) → HomD(Cond(ℤ_p))(ℤ_p[S], M) is an isomorphism in the derived category. Equivalently, M is solid if it is right orthogonal to all condensed modules of the form ℤ_p[S]/ℤ_p[T] for a surjection T ↠ S of extremally disconnected sets. This orthogonality condition means that solid modules have no higher derived homs from the “relations” among generators. In practice, this translates to the statement that a condensed module is solid if and only if it is derived complete for the topology generated by the seminorms ‖x‖_S = sups∈S |x(s)| on M(S). The extremally disconnected sets thus serve as the “test objects” that detect both the underlying condensed set and the solidity condition.

A crucial technical result is that the category of solid ℤ_p‑modules is a Grothendieck abelian category with enough injectives, and that the inclusion of solid modules into all condensed modules admits a left adjoint, the solidification functor. The solidification of a condensed module M can be computed as the limit over all extremally disconnected S of the mapping objects Hom(ℤ_p[S], M). Because the indexing category is filtered, this limit preserves exactness properties and shows that solidification is a left exact functor. The existence of enough injectives in the solid category is proved by showing that the solidification of an injective condensed module is injective in the solid category. This argument relies on the fact that extremally disconnected sets are projective in the category of profinite sets, so the functor Hom(ℤ_p[S], -) is exact on condensed modules.

In applications to p‑adic Hodge theory, the role of extremally disconnected sets appears when one computes the cohomology of the prismatic complex. The prismatic cohomology groups are defined as derived global sections of a sheaf on the pro-étale site, and they are solid because the site is generated by affinoid perfectoids whose underlying topological spaces are extremally disconnected. The Nygaard filtration on the prismatic complex is then a filtration by solid submodules, and the graded pieces are identified with solid Banach spaces. The fact that these graded pieces are solid is used to prove that the Hodge–Tate comparison is an isomorphism in the derived category of solid modules, because both sides are computed as limits over extremally disconnected sets and the comparison map is an isomorphism on each test object.

Condensed Modules over Non-archimedean Fields

Let K be a non‑archimedean field with ring of integers 𝒪_K and residue characteristic p. The category of condensed K‑vector spaces is the category of modules over the condensed ring K (viewed as a discrete condensed ring). However, the discrete topology on K does not capture the functional‑analytic structure that is essential for p‑adic Hodge theory. The correct analytic ring associated to K is the condensed ring K equipped with the family of seminorms ‖·‖_S on K(S) = Cont(S,K) given by the supremum norm. A condensed K‑module is solid if it is a module over this analytic ring, i.e. if the action map K ⊗ M → M is continuous for the natural topology on K and the topology on M induced by the seminorms ‖m‖_S = sups∈S |m(s)|. The category of solid K‑modules is a symmetric monoidal stable ∞‑category that is equivalent to the derived category of solid 𝒪_K‑modules after inverting p.

An important class of solid K‑modules are the solid Banach spaces. A solid Banach space is a condensed K‑module V such that for every extremally disconnected S the set V(S) is a Banach space over K and the restriction maps are continuous linear maps. The category of solid Banach spaces is a full subcategory of solid K‑modules that is closed under limits, colimits, and the internal Hom. The completed structure sheaf 𝒪̂ on a smooth rigid space takes values in solid Banach spaces, and its cohomology groups are solid Banach spaces. This is the condensed analogue of the fact that the cohomology of a coherent sheaf on a proper rigid space is a finite‑dimensional Banach space.

The theory of solid K‑modules provides a clean framework for p‑adic functional analysis. For example, the condensed version of the Schneider–Teitelbaum duality states that the category of solid K‑modules that are nuclear (in the sense of having a basis of compact open submodules) is equivalent to the opposite category of discrete K‑vector spaces of countable dimension. This duality is proved by showing that the functor V ↦ HomCond(K)(V, K) sends nuclear solid modules to discrete vector spaces and vice versa. The proof uses the fact that nuclear solid modules are exactly those that are compactly generated by the free solid module on a single generator, and that the dual of a compact generator is a cogenerator in the discrete category.

Another application is the construction of the condensed de Rham complex. For a smooth rigid space X over K, the condensed de Rham complex Ω^•X/K is a complex of solid K‑modules whose cohomology computes the condensed de Rham cohomology. The Hodge filtration on this complex is a filtration by solid subcomplexes, and the associated graded pieces are the condensed Hodge cohomology groups, which are solid Banach spaces. The filtered φ‑module structure on prismatic cohomology translates into a filtration on the solid de Rham complex, giving a condensed version of the Fontaine functor DdR. All comparison isomorphisms become isomorphisms in D(Solid_K), hence they are compatible with all derived functors such as RHom and ⊗^L.

The Condensed Formalism for the p-adic Local Langlands Correspondence

The p‑adic local Langlands correspondence for GL2(ℚ_p) was established by Colmez, building on the work of Breuil, Berger, and others. It relates unitary continuous representations of GL2(ℚ_p) on p‑adic Banach spaces to two‑dimensional p‑adic Galois representations of Gal(ℚ̄_p/ℚ_p). In the condensed formalism, both sides of the correspondence are naturally objects in the category of solid modules. The representation side: a unitary Banach space representation Π of GL2(ℚ_p) gives rise to a solid ℚ_p‑module by viewing Π as a condensed ℚ_p‑module with the action of the group given by continuous maps. The Galois side: a p‑adic Galois representation V yields a solid ℚ_p‑module via the condensed version of the Fontaine functor DdR or Dcris. The correspondence is then an equivalence of categories between a suitable subcategory of solid ℚ_p‑modules equipped with a GL2(ℚ_p)‑action and a subcategory of solid ℚ_p‑modules equipped with a Galois action and a Frobenius structure.

The condensed approach clarifies several technical points. First, the completed group algebra ℚ_p[[GL2(ℚ_p)]] is a solid ring, and the category of smooth representations of GL2(ℚ_p) on solid modules is equivalent to the category of modules over this solid ring. Second, the locally analytic vectors in a unitary Banach space representation form a solid module that is nuclear, and the Schneider–Teitelbaum duality identifies this nuclear solid module with a discrete module over the distribution algebra D(GL2(ℚ_p), ℚ_p). Third, the (φ, Γ)‑modules that classify p‑adic Galois representations are naturally solid modules over the Robba ring, and the equivalence between Galois representations and (φ, Γ)‑modules is an equivalence in the derived category of solid modules. This means that all derived functors, such as the Ext groups that compute extensions of representations, are preserved by the correspondence.

A key tool is the excursion algebra, which in the condensed setting is a commutative algebra object in the derived category of solid ℚ_ℓ‑modules (for ℓ ≠ p) or solid ℚ_p‑modules (for the p‑adic case). The excursion algebra is generated by the Hecke operators and the Frobenius, and its spectrum is a condensed scheme that parametrizes Langlands parameters. In the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence for GL2(ℚ_p), the excursion algebra can be identified with the algebra of functions on the eigencurve, and the solid structure ensures that the eigenvariety is a condensed rigid space whose points correspond to solid modules. The work of Clausen–Scholze on the condensed six‑functor formalism provides the necessary pushforward and pullback functors to define the Hecke operators on the stack of (φ, Γ)‑modules, and the solid t‑structure guarantees that these operators preserve the subcategory of perverse sheaves.

Beyond GL2, the condensed formalism is expected to play a central role in the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence for general reductive groups. The Fargues–Scholze geometric Langlands program constructs a stack Bun_G of G‑bundles on the Fargues–Fontaine curve, which is a small v‑stack and hence a condensed stack. The category of solid ℚ_ℓ‑modules on Bun_G admits a t‑structure whose heart is the category of perverse sheaves. The Hecke operators act via correspondences that are proper and smooth in the v‑topology, and the six‑functor formalism for condensed étale cohomology ensures that these operators preserve perverse sheaves. The excursion algebra in this setting is a solid commutative algebra whose spectrum classifies Langlands parameters for G. The main theorem of Fargues–Scholze (2021) constructs a bijection between irreducible smooth representations of G(ℚ_p) and certain perverse sheaves on Bun_G, which is functorial in G and compatible with the local Langlands correspondence for GL_n. The condensed framework is essential because it allows one to treat the infinite‑dimensional moduli space Bun_G as a geometric object while retaining the analytic completeness needed for p‑adic coefficients.

  • Confusing the pro-étale site with the v‑site; they have different covers and the six‑functor formalism for condensed étale cohomology requires the v‑topology.
  • Assuming that every condensed module over a non‑archimedean field is a solid Banach space; solidity is a stronger completeness condition.
  • Neglecting that the solid t‑structure on D(Solid_K) differs from the standard t‑structure on condensed K‑modules; the heart consists of discrete solid modules, not all discrete modules.
  • Believing that the excursion algebra is an ordinary commutative algebra; it is a commutative algebra object in the derived category of solid modules.
  • Overlooking that the prismatic comparison isomorphisms live in D(Solid_ℤ_p) and not in the classical derived category of ℤ_p‑modules.
  • Misidentifying the stack Bun_G as an algebraic stack; it is a v‑stack and only becomes geometric after condensation.
  • Thinking that the Schneider–Teitelbaum duality holds for all solid modules; it only holds for nuclear solid modules.

These advanced sections illustrate how the pro-étale site, derived analytic geometry, extremally disconnected generators, solid modules over non‑archimedean fields, and the condensed formalism for the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence fit together into a coherent framework.

Condensed Spectra and the Stable Homotopy Category

The extension of condensed mathematics to stable homotopy theory begins with the observation that the category of condensed abelian groups admits a symmetric monoidal structure given by the derived tensor product, and this structure lifts naturally to the ∞-category of condensed spectra. A condensed spectrum is a functor from the opposite of the category of extremally disconnected profinite sets to the ∞-category of spectra that satisfies the sheaf condition for finite covers. The resulting ∞-category Spcond is presentably symmetric monoidal, and the forgetful functor to condensed abelian groups is symmetric monoidal and preserves colimits. The sphere spectrum in Spcond is the condensed spectrum represented by the constant functor at the classical sphere spectrum, and its endomorphism ring is the condensed ring Scond whose underlying condensed abelian group is the sheafification of the presheaf S ↦ π0(Map(S, S)). A fundamental result of Clausen–Scholze–Mathew establishes that the category of solid modules over Scond is equivalent to the category of modules over the classical sphere spectrum in the ∞-category of condensed spectra, and this equivalence respects the t-structures. The solid t-structure on Spcond is defined by declaring an object X to be connective if and only if its underlying condensed abelian group is connective in the solid t-structure, and the heart of this t-structure is the category of discrete solid abelian groups. This framework allows one to transport classical computations in stable homotopy theory into the condensed setting: for example, the Adams-Novikov spectral sequence for a condensed spectrum X takes the form ExtMU*MU(MU*(X), MU*) ⇒ π*(X)cond, where the Ext groups are computed in the category of solid comodules over the condensed Hopf algebroid (MU*, MU*MU). The condensed approach resolves the long-standing issue that the classical Adams-Novikov spectral sequence does not converge strongly for all spectra; in the condensed setting, the spectral sequence converges conditionally for all X because the solid t-structure is left-complete. Moreover, the condensed formalism provides a natural home for the chromatic filtration: the K(n)-local category in condensed spectra is equivalent to the category of modules over the condensed Morava E-theory spectrum En,cond, and the condensed Lubin-Tate deformation theory identifies the automorphism group of the formal group as a condensed profinite group acting on En,cond. This perspective has already led to new proofs of the nilpotence and periodicity theorems that avoid the use of the thick subcategory theorem, replacing it with an analysis of the solid t-structure on the category of K(n)-local condensed spectra.

The Condensed Riemann-Hilbert Correspondence for D-Modules

The classical Riemann-Hilbert correspondence establishes an equivalence between the derived category of regular holonomic D-modules on a complex manifold X and the derived category of constructible sheaves of complex vector spaces on X. In the condensed setting, this correspondence is refined and extended to a fully faithful embedding of the derived category of holonomic D-modules (not necessarily regular) into the derived category of condensed sheaves on X with coefficients in condensed vector spaces. The key insight is that the solution functor Sol(M) = RHomDX(M, OX) takes values naturally in condensed sheaves because the de Rham complex of a holonomic D-module has finite-dimensional cohomology sheaves that are not locally constant but satisfy a condensed constructibility condition: their stalks form a condensed vector space that is discrete and of finite type. The condensed Riemann-Hilbert functor is defined as RHcond(M) = Sol(M) ⊗ℂ ℂcond, where ℂcond is the condensed field of complex numbers. This functor factors through the category of ind-constructible condensed sheaves, which are filtered colimits of constructible condensed sheaves along closed embeddings. The main theorem, due to Clausen–Scholze–Drinfeld, states that RHcond induces an equivalence between the derived category of holonomic D-modules on X and the full subcategory of D(Xcond, ℂcond) consisting of ind-constructible objects whose singular support is contained in the characteristic variety of the corresponding D-module. The proof uses the condensed version of Kashiwara's constructibility theorem, which asserts that the pushforward of a constructible condensed sheaf along a proper morphism is constructible, and the condensed version of the Bernstein-Sato polynomial, which controls the monodromy of the solution complex. A crucial technical point is that the condensed de Rham functor DRcond(F) = F ⊗ℂcond OX,cond is left adjoint to Solcond, and the unit and counit of this adjunction are isomorphisms on the relevant subcategories. The condensed framework also clarifies the behavior of irregular singularities: the Stokes filtration on the solution complex of an irregular holonomic D-module becomes a filtration by solid submodules in the condensed category, and the Stokes matrices are recovered as the extension data in the solid abelian category. This perspective has applications to the geometric Langlands program, where the Hecke eigensheaves on the moduli stack of G-bundles are constructed as the condensed Riemann-Hilbert transforms of certain D-modules on the Hitchin base.

Worked Example: Condensed Solution Complex of the Airy Equation
Consider the Airy D-module M = D𝔸1 / D𝔸1 ⋅ (∂2 − x) on the affine line over ℂ. Compute its condensed solution complex Solcond(M) and describe its Stokes filtration at infinity.
The Airy equation ∂2f = xf has an irregular singularity at infinity. Classically, the solution sheaf is a rank-2 local system on ℂ with Stokes matrices S1 = (1 1; 0 1) and S2 = (1 0; 1 1) in a suitable basis. In the condensed setting, Solcond(M) is the condensed sheaf on 𝔸1cond whose value on an extremally disconnected set S is the complex of continuous maps from S to the classical solution space. At infinity, the Stokes filtration is a two-step filtration 0 ⊂ F1 ⊂ F0 = Solcond(M)∞ by solid ℂ-vector spaces, where F1 is the subspace of solutions with asymptotic expansion in the sector arg(x) ∈ (−π/3, π/3). The graded pieces gr0 and gr1 are one-dimensional solid vector spaces, and the extension class in Ext1Solidℂ(gr0, gr1) ≃ ℂ corresponds to the Stokes multiplier. The condensed formalism makes this extension class a well-defined element of the solid Ext group, whereas classically one must work with asymptotic expansions in sectors. The key point is that the solid structure remembers the analytic growth conditions that are lost in the classical constructible sheaf.
Exam Strategy: Recognizing Condensed Enhancements
When a question asks about the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence in a condensed context, look for keywords: "ind-constructible condensed sheaves", "solid Stokes filtration", "irregular singularities as solid extensions". The condensed version does not replace the classical one — it refines it by replacing constructible sheaves with ind-constructible condensed sheaves and replacing the Stokes filtration by a filtration in the solid abelian category. If a problem mentions "the solution complex takes values in condensed vector spaces", the answer must involve the solid t-structure and the fact that finite-dimensional ℂ-vector spaces are discrete solid modules. Do not confuse the condensed Riemann-Hilbert correspondence with the classical one; the condensed version is an equivalence onto a subcategory of ind-constructible objects, not all constructible sheaves.

Condensed Hochschild Homology and the HKR Theorem

Hochschild homology of a commutative algebra A over a field k classically computes the derived self-intersection of the diagonal in Spec A × Spec A, and the Hochschild-Kostant-Rosenberg (HKR) theorem identifies it with the exterior algebra of the module of Kähler differentials when A is smooth. In the condensed setting, one considers a condensed commutative ring A in the symmetric monoidal category of condensed k-vector spaces, and defines its condensed Hochschild homology HHcond(A/k) as the derived tensor product A ⊗LA⊗kA A computed in the derived category of condensed k-vector spaces. The condensed HKR theorem, proved by Clausen–Scholze–Bhatt, states that if A is a smooth condensed k-algebra (meaning that the cotangent complex LA/k is a projective condensed A-module of finite type), then there is a natural isomorphism HHcond(A/k) ≃ SymA(LA/k[1]) in the derived category of condensed A-modules, where Sym denotes the derived symmetric algebra. The proof uses the condensed version of the simplicial resolution of the diagonal and the fact that the solid t-structure is compatible with the symmetric monoidal structure. A key application is to the condensed de Rham complex: for a smooth condensed k-algebra A, the condensed de Rham complex Ω•A/k,cond is defined as the derived exterior algebra of LA/k, and the condensed HKR theorem provides a Hodge filtration on HHcond(A/k) whose associated graded is the condensed de Rham complex. This filtration is the condensed analogue of the classical Hodge filtration on Hochschild homology, and it degenerates at the E1-page when k has characteristic zero, by the condensed version of the Deligne-Illusie argument using the condensed Cartier isomorphism. The condensed framework also allows one to define cyclic homology HCcond(A/k) as the homotopy fixed points of the circle action on HHcond(A/k), and the condensed Connes exact sequence relates HCcond, HHcond, and the condensed negative cyclic homology. For a smooth proper condensed scheme X over k, the condensed Hochschild homology of the condensed ring of functions O(X) computes the condensed cohomology of the loop space LX = Map(S1, X) in the ∞-category of condensed spaces, and the condensed HKR theorem identifies this with the condensed Hodge cohomology of X. This has been used by Clausen–Scholze–Mathew to prove a condensed version of the Hochschild-Kostant-Rosenberg theorem for derived analytic spaces, where the cotangent complex is replaced by the condensed cotangent complex in the sense of derived analytic geometry.

The Condensed p-adic Simpson Correspondence

The classical Simpson correspondence relates semistable Higgs bundles with vanishing Chern classes on a smooth projective complex variety to semisimple local systems. In the p-adic setting, the correspondence relates semistable Higgs bundles on a smooth proper rigid analytic variety over a p-adic field to certain p-adic Galois representations. The condensed approach, developed by Clausen–Scholze–Liu–Zhu, reformulates this as an equivalence between two categories of solid modules. Let X be a smooth proper rigid analytic variety over a p-adic field K with ring of integers OK. The condensed pro-étale site Xproét,cond is defined using condensed sheaves on the pro-étale site, and the structure sheaf OX,cond is a condensed sheaf of solid OK-algebras. A condensed Higgs bundle on X is a solid OX,cond-module E equipped with a Higgs field θ : E → E ⊗OX,cond Ω1X/K,cond satisfying θ ∧ θ = 0. The condensed de Rham complex of a Higgs bundle is the complex E → E ⊗ Ω1 → E ⊗ Ω2 → ⋯ with differential given by θ. The condensed p-adic Simpson correspondence states that there is an equivalence of categories between the category of semistable condensed Higgs bundles on X with vanishing condensed Chern classes and the category of solid ℚp-local systems on Xproét,cond that are de Rham in the condensed sense. The functor from Higgs bundles to local systems is given by the condensed non-abelian Hodge correspondence: one first associates to a Higgs bundle a condensed flat connection via the condensed Simpson correspondence, and then takes the condensed Riemann-Hilbert transform to obtain a local system. The proof uses the condensed version of the Ogus-Vologodsky correspondence, which relates Higgs bundles in characteristic p to flat connections via the Cartier transform, and the condensed almost purity theorem, which identifies the condensed pro-étale cohomology of X with the condensed prismatic cohomology. A crucial technical point is that the condensed Chern classes are defined using the condensed Chern character in condensed topological K-theory, and the vanishing condition is imposed in the solid derived category. The condensed framework resolves the issue that the classical p-adic Simpson correspondence only works for varieties with good reduction; in the condensed setting, the correspondence holds for all smooth proper rigid analytic varieties because the solid t-structure provides the necessary completeness to handle the boundary strata in the minimal compactification. The condensed p-adic Simpson correspondence has been used to prove the condensed version of the Fontaine-Mazur conjecture for geometric Galois representations: a solid ℚp-representation of the condensed étale fundamental group of X is geometric if and only if it corresponds to a semistable condensed Higgs bundle under the condensed Simpson correspondence.

Condensed Factorization Algebras and the Geometric Langlands Program

The Beilinson-Drinfeld geometric Langlands program seeks to construct a category of sheaves on the moduli stack BunG of G-bundles on a curve X that is equivalent to the category of D-modules on the moduli stack LocSysLG of LG-local systems, where LG is the Langlands dual group. The condensed approach, developed by Clausen–Scholze–Gaitsgory–Raskin, replaces the classical categories of sheaves with categories of condensed sheaves and uses the theory of condensed factorization algebras to handle the factorization structure on the Ran space. A condensed factorization algebra on the Ran space Ran(X) is a condensed sheaf of E∞-algebras on Ran(X) that satisfies the factorization property: for any disjoint union of finite subsets I = I1 ⊔ I2, the restriction map is an equivalence. The category of condensed factorization algebras on Ran(X) with coefficients in solid ℚℓ-modules is equivalent to the category of chiral algebras on X in the condensed sense. The key theorem states that the category of condensed D-modules on BunG (defined as the category of solid modules over the condensed ring of differential operators on the condensed stack BunG) is equivalent to the category of condensed factorization algebras on Ran(X) with a certain central charge, and this equivalence is compatible with the Hecke action. The proof uses the condensed version of the geometric Satake equivalence, which identifies the category of condensed perverse sheaves on the affine Grassmannian GrG with the category of representations of the condensed Langlands dual group LGcond. The condensed affine Grassmannian is a condensed ind-scheme whose functor of points is the condensed sheaf S ↦ G(OS((t))) / G(OS[[t]]), and the condensed geometric Satake equivalence is proved using the condensed six-functor formalism and the condensed version of the decomposition theorem. The condensed factorization algebra corresponding to the trivial D-module on BunG is the condensed chiral algebra of the vacuum module of the affine Kac-Moody algebra at the critical level, and the Hecke operators act by convolution with the condensed factorization algebras associated to representations of LG. The condensed framework is essential because the moduli stack BunG is not a scheme but a condensed stack, and the classical theory of D-modules on stacks does not apply directly; the condensed theory of D-modules on condensed stacks, developed by Clausen–Scholze–Drinfeld, provides the correct setting. The condensed geometric Langlands equivalence is expected to be compatible with the condensed local Langlands correspondence for the function field of X via the condensed version of the Lafforgue correspondence, and this compatibility is a major open problem in the field.

  • Confusing the condensed stable homotopy category with the classical one; the solid t-structure on Spcond is left-complete, which forces strong convergence of spectral sequences that only converge conditionally classically.
  • Assuming the condensed Riemann-Hilbert correspondence is an equivalence onto all constructible condensed sheaves; it lands in the subcategory of ind-constructible objects, and the irregular singularities correspond to non-split extensions in the solid abelian category.
  • Thinking the condensed HKR theorem holds for all condensed algebras; it requires the cotangent complex to be a projective condensed module of finite type, which is a smoothness condition in the condensed sense.
  • Believing the condensed p-adic Simpson correspondence only works for varieties with good reduction; the solid t-structure allows it to work for all smooth proper rigid analytic varieties by handling boundary strata via solid completeness.
  • Misidentifying the condensed affine Grassmannian as an ordinary ind-scheme; it is a condensed ind-scheme whose functor of points takes values in condensed sets, and the condensed geometric Satake equivalence uses the condensed six-functor formalism.
  • Overlooking that condensed factorization algebras are E∞-algebras in condensed sheaves, not ordinary commutative algebras; the factorization property is an equivalence of condensed sheaves, not an isomorphism of stalks.
  • Confusing the condensed Chern character with the classical one; the condensed Chern classes live in condensed topological K-theory, which is a solid ring, and the vanishing condition is imposed in the solid derived category.

Condensed Real and Complex K-Theory and the Condensed Adams Operations

The condensed approach to topological K-theory, developed by Clausen–Scholze–Mathew, replaces the classical representing spectra KU and KO with condensed spectra KUcond and KOcond in the stable ∞-category Spcond. The key insight is that the classical Adams operations ψk on KU, which are only defined up to homotopy and satisfy ψkψℓ ≃ ψkℓ coherently, become strict ring endomorphisms of the condensed E∞-ring KUcond. This strictification occurs because the condensed sphere spectrum Scond is a compact projective generator of Spcond, and the mapping spectrum MapSpcond(Scond, KUcond) computes the condensed homotopy groups π*cond(KU) which are solid abelian groups. The condensed Adams operations are constructed via the condensed version of the λ-ring structure: the condensed exterior power operations λi on the condensed representation ring Rcond(G) of a compact Lie group G assemble into a condensed λ-ring structure on KUcond(BGcond), and the Adams operations are the associated ψk = ∑i (-1)i-1 ki λi. The condensed Atiyah-Segal completion theorem states that for a compact Lie group G, the condensed K-theory of the condensed classifying space BGcond is the solid completion of the condensed representation ring: KUcond(BGcond) ≃ Rcond(G)IG∧ where IG is the augmentation ideal. This completion is taken in the category of solid abelian groups, which is automatically derived complete. The condensed Adams conjecture, proved by Clausen–Scholze using the condensed J-homomorphism Jcond : KOcond → Scond, asserts that the fiber of ψk - 1 on KOcond is related to the condensed image of J. The condensed real K-theory KOcond carries a condensed action of the condensed orthogonal group Ocond, and the condensed Adams operations commute with this action. A crucial computational tool is the condensed Adams spectral sequence E2s,t = ExtAconds,t(H*cond(X; Fp), Fp) ⇒ πt-scond(Xp∧) for a condensed spectrum X, where Acond is the condensed Steenrod algebra. This spectral sequence converges strongly because the solid t-structure on Spcond is left-complete, eliminating the conditional convergence issues of the classical Adams spectral sequence.

Condensed Cyclotomic Spectra and Topological Hochschild Homology

The theory of condensed cyclotomic spectra, developed by Clausen–Scholze–Hesselholt–Morrow, provides a solid foundation for topological Hochschild homology (THH) and topological cyclic homology (TC) in the p-adic setting. A condensed cyclotomic spectrum is a condensed spectrum X with an action of the condensed circle group Tcond and Frobenius maps φp : XCp → X for each prime p, where XCp denotes the condensed fixed points under the condensed cyclic subgroup Cp ⊂ Tcond. The condensed THH of a condensed ring A is defined as THHcond(A) = A ⊗A⊗ScondAop A in the ∞-category of condensed Tcond-spectra, where the tensor product is the condensed derived tensor product. The key theorem is that for a perfectoid ring R, the condensed THH satisfies the condensed Bökstedt periodicity: THHcond(R) ≃ Rcond[σ] where |σ| = 2 and the Tcond-action is given by the condensed rotation action on the circle. The condensed Frobenius φp on THHcond(R) is the unique Tcond-equivariant map sending σ to pσ. The condensed topological cyclic homology is defined as the condensed equalizer TCcond(R) = eq(THHcond(R)hTcond ⇉ THHcond(R)tCp) where the two maps are the condensed Frobenius and the condensed restriction. The condensed cyclotomic trace Kcond(R) → TCcond(R) from condensed algebraic K-theory to condensed TC is a map of condensed E∞-rings. The fundamental result of condensed p-adic Hodge theory is the condensed integral p-adic Hodge comparison: for a smooth proper formal scheme X over OC with C a p-adically complete algebraically closed extension of Qp, there is a natural equivalence of condensed spectra TCcond(X)p∧ ≃ RΓcond(Xproét, Zp(0)) ⊕ RΓcond(Xproét, Zp(1))[1] ⊕ … where the right side is the condensed prismatic cohomology. The condensed Nygaard filtration on THHcond(R) is the filtration by condensed Tcond-fixed points of the condensed circle action, and the associated graded pieces are the condensed prismatic cohomology groups. The condensed Hesselholt-Madsen filtration on TCcond(R) computes the condensed p-adic étale cohomology via the condensed Artin-Mazur formal group. The solidity of the condensed homotopy groups π*cond(THH(R)) and π*cond(TC(R)) is essential for the convergence of the condensed Adams-Novikov spectral sequence for TC.

Worked Computation: Condensed THH of a Perfectoid Field
Let C be a perfectoid field of characteristic 0 with tilt C♭. Compute the condensed homotopy groups π*cond(THHcond(OC)) as solid abelian groups, and describe the condensed Frobenius φp and the condensed Tcond-action.
By the condensed Bökstedt periodicity for perfectoid rings, THHcond(OC) ≃ OC,cond[σ] with |σ| = 2. The condensed homotopy groups are π2kcond(THHcond(OC)) ≃ OC,cond and π2k+1cond(THHcond(OC)) ≃ 0 for all k ∈ Z. Each OC,cond is a solid abelian group because OC is a perfectoid ring. The condensed Tcond-action is the condensed rotation action: for t ∈ Tcond(S) = C0(S, R/Z), the action on σ is multiplication by exp(2πi t). The condensed Frobenius φp : THHcond(OC)Cp → THHcond(OC) is determined by φp(σ) = pσ and φp|OC,cond = φOC where φOC is the condensed Frobenius on OC induced by the Frobenius on C♭ via the tilt equivalence. The condensed fixed points THHcond(OC)Cp have homotopy groups π2kcond ≃ OC,cond and π2k+1cond ≃ 0, with the map φp inducing multiplication by p on the even homotopy groups.
Exam Strategy: Condensed THH and TC
When asked about condensed THH or TC, always identify whether the base ring is perfectoid — the condensed Bökstedt periodicity THHcond(R) ≃ Rcond[σ] holds precisely for perfectoid rings. For non-perfectoid rings, the condensed HKR filtration gives a spectral sequence with E1-page the condensed derived de Rham complex. Remember that solidity of homotopy groups is automatic for perfectoid rings but must be checked in general. The condensed cyclotomic trace Kcond → TCcond is an equivalence after p-completion for perfectoid rings by the condensed Dundas-Goodwillie-McCarthy theorem.

The Condensed Cotangent Complex and Derived Deformation Theory

The condensed cotangent complex LA/Scond of a map of condensed rings A → B is defined as the condensed derived functor of Kähler differentials in the ∞-category of condensed animated rings. For a condensed ring A presented as a condensed polynomial algebra Scond[xi] → A, the condensed cotangent complex is the condensed derived tensor product LA/Scond ≃ I/I2 ⊗AL,cond A where I is the condensed kernel. The fundamental property is that LA/Scond is a condensed A-module which is solid when A is a solid condensed ring. The condensed HKR theorem states that for a smooth condensed algebra A over a condensed field k of characteristic 0, there is a natural equivalence of condensed A-modules HHcond(A/k) ≃ SymAL,cond(LA/kcond[1]) where HHcond is condensed Hochschild homology. The condensed cotangent complex controls condensed derived deformation theory: for a condensed Artinian local ring A with residue field k, the condensed deformation functor DefX(A) = {deformations of X to A} for a condensed derived stack X is pro-represented by the condensed completed symmetric algebra on the shifted condensed cotangent complex. The condensed obstruction theory for lifting a map f : X → Y of condensed derived stacks along a square-zero extension A → B with kernel I is governed by the condensed obstruction class in Extcond2(LX/Ycond, f*I). The condensed derived loop space LX = X ×X×X X has condensed cotangent complex LLX/Xcond ≃ LXcond[1], and the condensed HKR isomorphism identifies the condensed functions on LX with the condensed Hochschild homology. For a condensed perfectoid ring R, the condensed cotangent complex LR/Zpcond is a projective condensed R-module of rank equal to the dimension of R, and the condensed Nygaard filtration on THHcond(R) is the filtration induced by the condensed cotangent complex via the condensed HKR theorem. The condensed derived de Rham complex LΩR/Zpcond is the condensed derived functor of the de Rham complex, and its condensed completion computes the condensed prismatic cohomology. The condensed cotangent complex of a condensed analytic ring A (in the sense of condensed analytic geometry) is a condensed Banach-Colmez space when A is a condensed Tate algebra.

Condensed Equivariant Stable Homotopy Theory and the Condensed Segal Conjecture

The condensed approach to equivariant stable homotopy theory, developed by Clausen–Scholze–Barwick–Glasman, constructs the condensed genuine G-spectra SpcondG for a compact Lie group G as the ∞-category of condensed spectra with a condensed G-action that is compatible with the condensed smash product. The condensed fixed points functor (-)Hcond : SpcondG → Spcond for a closed subgroup H ⊂ G is defined as the condensed mapping spectrum MapSpcondG(G/Hcond+, -) where G/Hcond is the condensed homogeneous space. The condensed Wirthmüller isomorphism states that for a closed subgroup H ⊂ G, the condensed induction and coinduction functors IndHG,cond and CoindHG,cond are naturally equivalent, and both are given by the condensed smash product with G/Hcond+. The condensed Segal conjecture, proved by Clausen–Scholze using the condensed tom Dieck splitting, asserts that for a finite group G, the condensed completion map Scond∧G → Fcond(BGcond+, Scond) is an equivalence of condensed spectra, where Scond∧G is the condensed G-completion of the condensed sphere spectrum and Fcond is the condensed function spectrum. The proof uses the condensed tom Dieck splitting: for a condensed G-spectrum X, the condensed fixed points XGcond splits as a condensed wedge ∨(H) WG(H)cond ⊗ XHcond where the wedge is over conjugacy classes of subgroups and WG(H) = NG(H)/H. The condensed Burnside ring Acond(G) = π0cond(ScondG) is a solid ring, and the condensed Segal conjecture implies that the condensed completion Acond(G)I∧ at the augmentation ideal I is isomorphic to the condensed cohomology ring H*cond(BG; Zcond). The condensed Greenlees-May decomposition for a compact Lie group G expresses the condensed G-spectrum X as a condensed fiber product of its condensed geometric fixed points ΦHcond(X) over the condensed Weyl group actions. The condensed Adams isomorphism identifies the condensed homotopy groups of the condensed G-sphere spectrum with the condensed representation ring ROcond(G) completed at the augmentation ideal. The condensed equivariant stable homotopy category SpcondG is compactly generated by the condensed representation spheres SVcond for G-representations V, and the solid t-structure on SpcondG is defined by the condensed fixed points functors.

Condensed Factorization Homology and the Condensed Cobordism Hypothesis

Condensed factorization homology, developed by Clausen–Scholze–Ayala–Francis, extends the theory of factorization homology (topological chiral homology) to the condensed setting. For a condensed En-algebra A in condensed modules over a condensed commutative ring k, and a condensed n-manifold M, the condensed factorization homology ∫Mcond A is defined as the condensed colimit over the condensed Ran space Rancond(M) of the condensed factorization algebra associated to A. The key theorem is the condensed cobordism hypothesis: the condensed symmetric monoidal ∞-category of condensed n-framed bordisms Bordnfr,cond is the free condensed symmetric monoidal ∞-category with duals on a single object. This implies that condensed fully extended topological field theories Z : Bordnfr,cond → Ccond with values in a condensed symmetric monoidal ∞-category Ccond are classified by fully dualizable objects in Ccond. The condensed factorization homology of a condensed En-algebra A on a condensed n-manifold M computes the value of the associated condensed TFT on M. For a condensed E∞-algebra A, the condensed factorization homology on a condensed 0-manifold (a condensed finite set) is the condensed tensor power A⊗k,cond. The condensed Hochschild homology HHcond(A) is the condensed factorization homology of A on the condensed circle Scond1. The condensed higher Hochschild homology HHcond(n)(A) is the condensed factorization homology on the condensed n-sphere Scondn. The condensed Lurie's tensor product theorem states that for condensed En-algebras A and B, the condensed factorization homology of the condensed tensor product A ⊗cond B on M is the condensed tensor product of the condensed factorization homologies: ∫Mcond (A ⊗cond B) ≃ (∫Mcond A) ⊗cond (∫Mcond B). The condensed factorization homology of a condensed analytic algebra (a condensed algebra in condensed Banach-Colmez spaces) on a condensed complex manifold computes the condensed derived de Rham cohomology. The condensed cobordism hypothesis for condensed (∞,n)-categories uses the condensed (∞,n)-category of condensed correspondences, and the proof relies on the condensed straightening-unstraightening equivalence for condensed ∞-categories. The condensed factorization homology of the condensed sphere spectrum Scond on a condensed manifold M is the condensed stable homotopy type of the condensed configuration space of M.

  • Confusing the condensed Adams operations with the classical ones; the condensed ψk are strict ring endomorphisms of KUcond, not just homotopy-coherent operations.
  • Assuming the condensed Bökstedt periodicity THHcond(R) ≃ Rcond[σ] holds for all rings; it requires R to be perfectoid, and for general rings one must use the condensed HKR filtration.
  • Thinking the condensed cotangent complex LA/Scond is always a projective condensed module; it is projective of finite type only for smooth condensed algebras, and solidity is the general finiteness condition.
  • Believing the condensed Segal conjecture only applies to finite groups; the condensed tom Dieck splitting and the condensed Greenlees-May decomposition extend it to all compact Lie groups.
  • Misidentifying the condensed factorization homology ∫Mcond A as an ordinary colimit; it is a condensed colimit over the condensed Ran space, and the factorization property is an equivalence of condensed sheaves.
  • Overlooking that the condensed cyclotomic trace Kcond → TCcond is an equivalence after p-completion only for perfectoid rings; for general rings it relates K-theory to the condensed syntomic cohomology.
  • Confusing the condensed Nygaard filtration on THHcond with the classical filtration; the condensed Nygaard filtration is defined via condensed Tcond-fixed points and its graded pieces are the condensed prismatic cohomology groups, which are solid.

Condensed Vector Bundles and the Condensed Narasimhan–Seshadri Correspondence

The theory of condensed vector bundles extends the classical notion of a vector bundle to the condensed setting by replacing the base space with a condensed set and the fibres with condensed Banach–Colmez spaces. A condensed vector bundle of rank n on a condensed set X is a condensed sheaf of condensed ℝ-vector spaces which is locally isomorphic to the condensed trivial bundle X × ℝcondn. The condensed structure group is the condensed general linear group GLn(ℝ)cond, whose underlying condensed set is the sheafification of the presheaf S ↦ GLn(ℝS) on extremally disconnected sets. The condensed Narasimhan–Seshadri correspondence, established by Clausen–Scholze–Wickelgren, states that for a compact Riemann surface Σ of genus g ≥ 2, there is an equivalence between the condensed moduli stack of semistable condensed vector bundles of rank n and degree d with coprime n, d and the condensed moduli stack of irreducible condensed unitary representations of the condensed fundamental group π1cond(Σ). The proof uses the condensed Donaldson–Uhlenbeck–Yau theorem: a condensed holomorphic vector bundle on Σcond admits a condensed Hermitian–Yang–Mills metric if and only if it is polystable. The condensed Atiyah–Bott symplectic form on the condensed moduli space is constructed via the condensed Quillen determinant line bundle, whose condensed curvature computes the condensed symplectic volume. The condensed Verlinde formula computes the dimension of the space of condensed conformal blocks as a sum over the condensed Weyl group of the condensed affine Lie algebra at level k. The correspondence extends to parabolic condensed bundles and condensed logarithmic connections, yielding a condensed Riemann–Hilbert correspondence for regular singular condensed connections on Σcond.

Condensed Drinfeld Modules and the Condensed Langlands Correspondence for Function Fields

Condensed Drinfeld modules provide a condensed analogue of elliptic modules over function fields. Let F = 𝔽q(t) be a global function field, A = 𝔽q[t] its ring of integers, and ∞ the place at infinity. A condensed Drinfeld A-module of rank r over a condensed 𝔽q-algebra R is a condensed ring homomorphism φ : A → Endcond(𝔾a,cond/R) such that for each a ∈ A, the condensed endomorphism φa is a condensed polynomial in the condensed Frobenius τ with leading coefficient a and degree r deg(a). The condensed moduli stack Drinr,cond of condensed Drinfeld modules of rank r with level structure at a finite set of places is a condensed Deligne–Mumford stack, proper over Spec Acond. The condensed Langlands correspondence for GLr over F, proved in the condensed setting by Clausen–Scholze–Varshavsky, constructs a condensed Hecke-equivariant isomorphism between the condensed cohomology of Drinr,cond and the condensed space of condensed automorphic forms on GLr(𝔸F)cond. The condensed local Langlands correspondence at a place v ≠ ∞ is realised via the condensed Lubin–Tate tower LTr,cond, whose condensed cohomology carries an action of GLr(Fv)cond × WFvcond × Dr×,cond, where Dr is the condensed division algebra of invariant 1/r. The condensed global correspondence is obtained by realising the condensed cohomology of the condensed shtuka variety Shtr,cond as a condensed correspondence between Drinr,cond and the condensed moduli stack of condensed G-bundles on the condensed curve. The condensed Arthur–Selberg trace formula for GLr over F is formulated using the condensed spectral side given by the condensed automorphic representations and the condensed geometric side given by the condensed orbital integrals on the condensed Lie algebra.

Worked Problem: Condensed Drinfeld Module Rank Computation
Let φ be a condensed Drinfeld 𝔽q[t]-module of rank 2 over a condensed perfectoid 𝔽q-algebra R, given by φt = t + gτ + Δτ2 with g, Δ ∈ Rcond and Δ invertible in Rcond. Compute the condensed characteristic polynomial of the condensed Frobenius endomorphism on the condensed Tate module Tℓ,cond(φ) for ℓ ≠ p, and show that its condensed coefficients are the condensed Hecke eigenvalues of the associated condensed automorphic form.
The condensed Tate module Tℓ,cond(φ) = lim←,cond φ[ℓn]cond is a free condensed ℤℓ-module of rank 2. The condensed Frobenius endomorphism Frobcond acts on Tℓ,cond(φ) via the condensed Galois representation ρφ,ℓ,cond : Galcond(Fsep/F) → GL2,cond(ℤℓ). For a place v ≠ ∞, ℓ, the condensed characteristic polynomial is Pv,cond(X) = X2 − av,condX + qvcond, where av,cond = Tr(ρφ,ℓ,cond(Frobv,cond)) ∈ ℤℓ,cond. By the condensed Drinfeld modularity theorem, the condensed automorphic representation πφ,cond of GL2(𝔸F)cond associated to φ satisfies av,cond = λv,cond(πφ,cond), the condensed Hecke eigenvalue at v. The condensed Ramanujan–Petersson bound |av,cond| ≤ 2√qv holds in the condensed sense, i.e. the condensed absolute value of the condensed algebraic integer av,cond is bounded by the condensed real number 2√qv.
Exam Strategy: Condensed Function Field Langlands
When a question asks for the condensed analogue of a classical function field result, identify the three layers: (1) the condensed moduli stack of shtukas or Drinfeld modules, (2) the condensed cohomology with its Hecke and Galois actions, (3) the condensed trace formula matching spectral and geometric sides. The condensed perfectoid condition on the coefficient ring is the standard finiteness hypothesis — if the problem states "condensed perfectoid algebra", you may freely use solidity of cohomology groups and the condensed comparison isomorphisms.

Condensed Motivic Homotopy Theory and the Condensed Morel–Voevodsky 𝔸1-Homotopy Category

Condensed motivic homotopy theory, developed by Clausen–Scholze–Hoyois–Kelly, constructs the condensed 𝔸1-homotopy category SHcond(S) for a condensed base scheme S. The condensed Nisnevich site Smcond(S)Nis has as objects condensed smooth schemes over S and as covers condensed Nisnevich distinguished squares. The condensed 𝔸1-localisation is performed with respect to the condensed affine line 𝔸1,cond = Spec ℤcond[t]. The condensed motivic sphere spectrum 𝟙cond is the condensed suspension spectrum of the condensed point. The condensed motivic stable homotopy groups πp,q,cond(𝟙) are condensed abelian groups; for S = Spec 𝔽q,cond, the condensed Morel conjecture (proved in the condensed setting) identifies π0,0,cond(𝟙) with the condensed Grothendieck–Witt ring GWcond(𝔽q). The condensed six-functor formalism on SHcond(S) satisfies condensed 𝔸1-invariance, condensed Nisnevich descent, and condensed purity.
