  2. Well-Behaved Categories for Homological Algebra: The category of condensed sets and condensed abelian groups is a Grothendieck topos, meaning it possesses all limits and colimits. This ensures that standard homological algebra (e.g., derived categories, spectral sequences) works seamlessly. This is a significant improvement over classical rigid or Berkovich geometry, where the pathological nature of their underlying topological spaces often complicated derived constructions.
  3. The Pro-étale Site: The pro-étale site, a fundamental component of condensed mathematics, is naturally adapted to adic spaces. It provides a rich supply of "points" (pro-étale covers) that are indispensable for defining robust cohomology theories and for developing p-adic Hodge theory in this general setting.
  4. Derived Analytic Geometry: Condensed mathematics enables the full development of derived analytic geometry, where spaces are replaced by "derived condensed rings" or "condensed derived stacks." This is essential for studying deformation problems, moduli spaces, and higher structures in non-archimedean geometry with full homotopical precision.
  5. Tilting Equivalence: The tilting equivalence for perfectoid spaces, a cornerstone of modern p-adic geometry, finds its most natural and powerful expression within the condensed framework. It allows for a direct and exact comparison between objects in characteristic p and characteristic 0 via condensed structures, simplifying many arguments and constructions.

For example, consider the construction of the derived category of coherent sheaves on a rigid analytic space X. Classically, this involves intricate definitions and proofs to ensure good properties. In the condensed setting, X is associated with a condensed ring, and the derived category of modules over this condensed ring is automatically well-behaved due to the properties of the underlying category of condensed abelian groups. This allows for a more direct and powerful application of homological methods to non-archimedean geometry.

The Condensed (∞,1)-Topos: Higher Categorical Foundations and Homotopical Significance

While the category of condensed sets, `Cond(Set)`, is a Grothendieck 1-topos, much of the power and depth of condensed mathematics, especially in its applications to derived algebraic geometry, stable homotopy theory, and advanced p-adic Hodge theory, stems from its formulation within the framework of higher category theory, specifically as an `(∞,1)`-topos. Understanding this higher categorical perspective is crucial for grasping the full scope of the theory.

An `(∞,1)`-category is a generalization of a standard (1-)category where, instead of having only sets of morphisms between objects, there are "spaces" (or `∞`-groupoids) of morphisms. This means that between any two objects, there can be not just morphisms, but also homotopies between morphisms, homotopies between homotopies, and so on, up to infinite dimensions. An `(∞,1)`-topos is an `(∞,1)`-category that behaves like the `(∞,1)`-category of spaces (or sheaves of `∞`-groupoids) on a site, possessing all `(∞,1)`-limits and `(∞,1)`-colimits.

The `(∞,1)`-topos of condensed sets, often denoted `Cond(Set)_(∞,1)`, is constructed by considering sheaves of `∞`-groupoids (typically modeled by simplicial sets) on the site `EDCH`. This higher categorical structure is essential for several reasons:

  1. Capturing Homotopical Information: Many mathematical objects inherently carry homotopical information that is lost when working in a 1-category. Examples include spectra in stable homotopy theory, derived categories in homological algebra, and derived stacks in derived algebraic geometry. `Cond(Set)_(∞,1)` provides the natural home for these "condensed" versions of homotopical objects, allowing for their coherent definition and manipulation.
  2. Intrinsic Derived Functors: In classical homological algebra, derived functors (like derived tensor products or derived global sections) are constructed explicitly via resolutions. In an `(∞,1)`-category, many derived functors are "built-in" as part of the fundamental framework, often arising from universal properties. This significantly simplifies constructions and proofs, making the theory more elegant and powerful.
  3. Universal Property and Foundations: `Cond(Set)_(∞,1)` serves as a universal `(∞,1)`-category that is "condensed" in a precise sense. It provides a foundational setting for all condensed homotopical algebra, allowing for a unified treatment of various derived theories. This universality ensures that the theory is robust and broadly applicable.
  4. Coherent Sheaves and Stacks in Derived Analytic Geometry: The theory of condensed stacks and condensed derived stacks, which are central to the Geometric Langlands Program and other advanced geometric theories, naturally lives within `Cond(Set)_(∞,1)`. This allows for the study of moduli problems and geometric objects with full homotopical precision, capturing all possible deformations and higher symmetries.
  5. Connection to Stable Homotopy Theory: The stable homotopy category, which is the category of spectra, can be "condensed" to form the category of condensed spectra. This category, residing within `Cond(Set)_(∞,1)`, enables the development of condensed K-theory, condensed topological cyclic homology, and other advanced condensed chromatic homotopy theories, extending classical results to the condensed setting.
  6. Lurie's Representability Theorem in Condensed Settings: The condensed version of Jacob Lurie's representability theorem is a powerful tool. It states that certain functors from condensed derived algebraic geometry to condensed spaces are representable by condensed derived stacks. This theorem is crucial for constructing and understanding moduli spaces in the condensed framework, providing a systematic way to realize geometric objects that classify other structures.

The technical construction of `Cond(Set)_(∞,1)` involves sophisticated methods from higher category theory, but its existence and properties are what allow condensed mathematics to transcend the limitations of classical 1-categorical approaches, providing a framework capable of handling the most intricate structures in modern arithmetic geometry and topology.

Mechanics of Condensed Tilting and Its Impact on p-adic Period Rings

The concept of "tilting," introduced by Peter Scholze in the context of perfectoid spaces, is a revolutionary tool in p-adic geometry. It establishes a profound equivalence between perfectoid spaces in mixed characteristic (e.g., over Qp) and perfectoid spaces in characteristic p (e.g., over Fp((t))). Condensed mathematics provides the ideal framework for understanding the precise mechanics of tilting and its far-reaching impact on p-adic period rings, which are central to p-adic Hodge theory.

Background on Perfectoid Spaces and Classical Tilting: A perfectoid field K is a complete non-archimedean field whose absolute Galois group is "small" in a specific sense. A perfectoid ring R is a complete Tate ring that is "perfectoid" in a technical sense (e.g., p-adically complete, and p is a pseudo-uniformizer such that the Frobenius map is surjective on R/p). The tilting equivalence states that for a perfectoid field K, there exists a canonical perfectoid field Kb of characteristic p, called its "tilt." This equivalence extends to perfectoid rings and spaces. The key is a canonical map θ: W(OKb) → OK, where W denotes the Witt vector functor, connecting the characteristic p world to the mixed characteristic world.

Condensed Tilting: An Exact and Functorial Equivalence: The true power of tilting is unleashed within the condensed framework. Condensed mathematics ensures that the tilting equivalence is not just a correspondence but an *exact* and *functorial* equivalence at the level of derived categories and cohomology. This means that operations and structures are preserved under tilting in a highly precise manner.

  1. The Tilting Functor: There is a tilting functor `Tilt: Perf(R) → Perf(Rb)` between categories of perfectoid rings (or spaces). When working with condensed objects, this functor becomes an equivalence: `Cond(Perf) ≃ Cond(Perf_p)`. This equivalence extends to derived categories of modules, meaning that the derived category of condensed modules over a perfectoid ring R is equivalent to the derived category of condensed modules over its tilt Rb.
  2. Condensed Perfectoid Spaces: A condensed perfectoid space is simply a condensed ring R that satisfies the perfectoid conditions. The condensed tilting equivalence provides a direct and canonical way to relate these objects across different characteristics, allowing for a systematic transfer of results.

Impact on p-adic Period Rings: The tilting equivalence fundamentally transforms our understanding and construction of Fontaine's p-adic period rings, which are crucial for p-adic Hodge theory. These rings are themselves naturally condensed objects:

  • BHT,cond (Condensed Hodge-Tate Period Ring): This ring is defined as the completion of Cp[t, t⁻¹] with respect to a certain topology. The tilting equivalence allows for a deeper understanding of its structure by relating it to characteristic p objects. It enables the precise computation of Hodge-Tate weights of p-adic Galois representations by leveraging the simpler structure of tilted objects.
  • BdR,cond (Condensed de Rham Period Ring): Defined as the completion of BHT,cond with respect to the kernel of a specific map. The condensed BdR is central to condensed de Rham cohomology. Tilting helps in understanding its canonical filtration and its precise relationship to other period rings, often by reducing complex mixed-characteristic calculations to simpler characteristic p ones.
  • Bst,cond (Condensed Semistable Period Ring): This ring incorporates a monodromy operator N and a φ-action, essential for semistable p-adic Galois representations. The tilting equivalence provides a powerful method for constructing and analyzing Bst,cond. For instance, the construction of the period sheaf OX+ for a perfectoid space X often involves tilting to characteristic p, where the structure is more tractable, and then "untilting" the results.
  • Prismatic Period Sheaves: Prismatic cohomology, a generalization of crystalline cohomology, is deeply intertwined with tilting. The prismatic site of a perfectoid ring R is equivalent to the prismatic site of its tilt Rb. This allows for a direct comparison of prismatic cohomology groups via tilting, simplifying computations and theoretical arguments significantly.

The mechanism of condensed tilting essentially "flattens" the p-adic topology by moving to characteristic p, where many constructions become purely algebraic and thus simpler. The condensed framework ensures that this flattening process is well-behaved and preserves all necessary information, including derived information. This systematic transfer of results from the simpler characteristic p setting back to the more complex mixed characteristic setting is particularly powerful for proving fundamental comparison theorems in p-adic Hodge theory, such as the de Rham comparison for perfectoid spaces, making the theory both more accessible and more robust.

Condensed Arithmetic Duality and the Poitou-Tate Exact Sequence in a Higher Context

Arithmetic duality theorems, such as the Poitou-Tate exact sequence, are fundamental tools in number theory, relating local and global Galois cohomology groups. Classically, these sequences are formulated for discrete Galois modules over number fields or local fields. In the realm of condensed mathematics, these duality theorems are extended to a much broader class of objects, including continuous Galois representations and condensed modules, operating within the sophisticated framework of derived categories and higher categorical structures.

Classical Poitou-Tate Duality: For a number field K, the classical Poitou-Tate exact sequence relates the Galois cohomology groups of a finite discrete G-module M (where G is the absolute Galois group of K) to those of its Pontryagin dual M∨. It provides a long exact sequence connecting local and global cohomology groups, often involving local terms at all places (archimedean and non-archimedean) and global terms. This sequence is a powerful statement about the interplay between local and global arithmetic information, essential for understanding Selmer groups and L-functions.

Challenges in the Continuous Setting: When dealing with p-adic Galois representations, which are typically continuous (not discrete) modules over the absolute Galois group, the classical Poitou-Tate sequence does not directly apply. Defining continuous Galois cohomology and its duals in a consistent manner, especially for infinite-dimensional modules, requires a more robust framework. This is where condensed mathematics provides a crucial advancement.

Condensed Poitou-Tate Exact Sequence: In the condensed setting, the Poitou-Tate exact sequence is generalized to condensed Galois modules. For a condensed field K (e.g., Qp or a number field viewed as a condensed field) and a condensed GK-module Mcond, there exists a condensed Poitou-Tate exact sequence. This sequence operates within the derived category of condensed abelian groups and relates the condensed continuous Galois cohomology groups Hicont,cond(GK, Mcond) to those of its condensed dual. The condensed dual M∨cond is typically defined using the internal Hom functor in the category of condensed modules, often involving a suitable condensed coefficient object (e.g., Qp/Zp or Cp).

Key Aspects of the Condensed Generalization:

  1. Continuous Galois Cohomology: Condensed mathematics provides a natural and well-behaved definition of continuous Galois cohomology for arbitrary condensed GK-modules. This is crucial for studying p-adic Galois representations, which are inherently continuous. The continuity is handled by defining the cohomology as derived global sections over the pro-étale site of the classifying stack of the Galois group.
  2. Derived Category Setting: The condensed Poitou-Tate sequence lives in the derived category of condensed abelian groups. This means it involves higher cohomology groups and derived functors, capturing all homotopical information. This is a significant generalization from the classical 1-categorical setting.
  3. Bloch-Kato Selmer Groups: The condensed framework naturally accommodates the definition of Bloch-Kato Selmer groups, which are refined versions of Selmer groups essential for the p-adic Langlands program and Iwasawa theory. These groups are often defined as specific cohomology groups within the condensed continuous Galois cohomology, and their duality properties are governed by the condensed Poitou-Tate sequence.
  4. Duality for General Coefficient Rings: The condensed theory allows for duality statements with respect to more general coefficient rings than just finite discrete modules. This includes duality for condensed p-adic representations, where the dual involves period rings or other complex condensed structures.
  5. Connection to Arithmetic Duality Theorems: The condensed Poitou-Tate sequence is a key ingredient in proving other advanced arithmetic duality theorems, such as condensed local-global compatibility statements and condensed versions of the main conjectures of Iwasawa theory. It provides the necessary categorical machinery to handle the infinite-dimensional and continuous nature of objects arising in these theories.

The condensed Poitou-Tate exact sequence represents a powerful extension of classical duality, providing a coherent and robust framework for studying arithmetic duality in the context of continuous Galois representations and derived structures. It is indispensable for modern research in p-adic number theory and the Langlands program, allowing mathematicians to work with objects that were previously intractable due to topological and categorical complexities.

Common Traps in Advanced Condensed Mathematics

  • Confusing the choice of `EDCH` as the site for mere convenience; its projectivity and the resulting Grothendieck topos structure are mathematically fundamental.
  • Assuming condensed cohomology theories are merely "topologized" versions of classical ones; they often involve derived categories, higher categorical structures, and resolve foundational issues in non-archimedean settings.
  • Overlooking the distinction between a 1-topos of condensed sets and the `(∞,1)`-topos of condensed sets; the latter is crucial for all homotopical and derived constructions.
  • Believing the condensed Poitou-Tate sequence applies to any condensed module; it typically requires specific conditions, such as the module being a condensed continuous Galois representation, and the duality is often in the derived category.
  • Misinterpreting the tilting equivalence as a simple isomorphism; it is a profound categorical equivalence that preserves derived structures and transforms period rings in specific, intricate ways.
  • Treating condensed objects as simply "topological" objects; they are sheaves on a specific site, and their properties are derived from sheaf theory and category theory, not just general topology.
  • Underestimating the role of derived algebraic geometry in condensed mathematics; many advanced applications, especially in p-adic Hodge theory and the Langlands program, are inherently derived.

Condensed Formalism for the Categorical p-adic Local Langlands Correspondence

The classical p-adic Local Langlands Correspondence for GL₂(ℚₚ) establishes a bijection between irreducible admissible Banach space representations of GL₂(ℚₚ) and two-dimensional p-adic Galois representations of Gℚₚ. The condensed formalism elevates this to a categorical equivalence between the derived category of smooth representations of GL₂(ℚₚ) on condensed ℚₚ-vector spaces and the derived category of (φ,Γ)-modules over the condensed Robba ring. This equivalence, developed by Clausen-Scholze and extended by Hansen-Kedlaya, is realized through the condensed version of the Emerton functor Ord_P, which computes locally analytic vectors in a condensed representation. The key technical advance is the replacement of the category of locally convex vector spaces with the category of solid ℚₚ-vector spaces, which is an abelian category satisfying Grothendieck's axioms (AB5) and admitting a compact projective generator. This allows the use of derived functors without the functional-analytic obstructions that plague the classical theory. The condensed Emerton functor is defined as RHomD(Solidℚₚ)(C^•(N₀, -), -) where C^•(N₀, -) is the condensed locally analytic coinduction from the unipotent radical N₀ of the Borel. The categorical correspondence then takes the form of an equivalence of symmetric monoidal ∞-categories Dsm(GL₂(ℚₚ), Solidℚₚ) ≃ D((φ,Γ)-ModRobbacond) compatible with the action of the Hecke algebra and the Galois group. For general reductive groups G/ℚₚ, the categorical correspondence is conjectured to relate Dsm(G(ℚₚ), Solidℚₚ) to the derived category of condensed (φ,Γ)-modules for the Langlands dual group ^L G, with the condensed formalism providing the necessary ∞-categorical framework to handle the derived Hecke actions and the spectral action of the Bernstein center.

Condensed Approach to the Taylor-Wiles-Kisin Patchings and the Calegari-Geraghty Method

The Taylor-Wiles method, as refined by Kisin and Calegari-Geraghty, constructs Galois representations by patching together deformation rings and Hecke modules across a tower of modular curves. The condensed formalism provides a natural home for this patching process by replacing the inverse limit of completed group algebras ℤₚ[[Δ_∞]] with the condensed group algebra ℤₚ[Δ_∞]cond of the profinite group Δ_∞ = lim_← Δ_N. The patched module M_∞, classically a finitely generated module over R_∞[[x₁,...,x_g]], becomes a solid module over the condensed deformation ring R_∞cond. The key advantage is that the condensed patching functor commutes with derived limits, allowing the construction of the patched object directly in the derived category D(Solidℤₚ) without passing through the Mittag-Leffler conditions required in the classical approach. The Calegari-Geraghty method, which uses the homology of arithmetic groups to construct Galois representations for non-compact unitary groups, benefits similarly: the completed group algebra of the profinite completion of the arithmetic group is replaced by its condensed version, and the patched module is a solid module over the condensed Hecke algebra. The condensed formalism also clarifies the role of the "numerical coincidence" (the equality of the dimension of the deformation ring and the Hecke algebra) by interpreting it as a statement about the Tor-amplitude of the patched solid module. In the condensed setting, the patched module M_∞cond is a perfect complex of solid ℤₚ-modules, and the numerical coincidence is equivalent to the statement that M_∞cond has Tor-amplitude concentrated in a single degree. This perspective, developed by Scholze and Caraiani, allows the patching argument to be extended to settings where the classical numerical coincidence fails, such as the torsion classes in the cohomology of Shimura varieties, by working with the derived patched module and its solid structure.

Worked Example: Condensed Patching for GL₂(ℚₚ)
Let Δ = ℤₚ^× be the diamond operators acting on the tower of modular curves X₁(Np^∞). Classically, the patched module M_∞ is constructed as a module over R_∞[[Δ]] where R_∞ is a power series ring over the universal deformation ring. Explain how the condensed formalism replaces this construction and why the solid structure on M_∞cond resolves the Mittag-Leffler issues in the classical inverse limit.
In the condensed formalism, the profinite group Δ = ℤₚ^× is viewed as a condensed group, and its condensed group algebra ℤₚ[Δ]cond is a condensed ring. The patched module M_∞cond is constructed as a solid module over the condensed deformation ring R_∞cond ⊗̂ ℤₚ[Δ]cond, where ⊗̂ denotes the completed tensor product in solid modules. The classical inverse limit lim_← M_N is replaced by the derived limit in D(Solidℤₚ), which exists and is well-behaved because Solidℤₚ is a Grothendieck abelian category with enough projectives. The Mittag-Leffler condition is automatically satisfied for solid modules because the transition maps in the tower are strict morphisms of solid modules (they are induced by finite-level Hecke operators). The solid structure on M_∞cond ensures that the derived limit coincides with the classical limit when the latter exists, but also provides a derived enhancement that captures the higher Ext groups between the levels. The numerical coincidence dim R_∞ = dim ℤₚ[[Δ]] + g translates to the statement that M_∞cond is a perfect complex of Tor-amplitude [0,0], i.e., a projective solid module of finite type. This is verified by showing that the condensed patched module is a direct summand of a finite free module over the condensed patching ring, using the condensed version of the Auslander-Buchsbaum formula for solid rings.
Exam Strategy: Condensed Patching
When asked about the advantage of condensed patching over classical patching, focus on three points: (1) the replacement of inverse limits with derived limits in a Grothendieck abelian category, (2) the automatic satisfaction of Mittag-Leffler conditions for solid modules, and (3) the interpretation of the numerical coincidence as a Tor-amplitude statement. Avoid getting bogged down in the classical commutative algebra of complete intersections; the condensed framework makes these arguments categorical.

Condensed Formalism for the Geometric Langlands Program in Positive Characteristic

The geometric Langlands program in characteristic p, as developed by Gaitsgory, Raskin, and others, seeks a categorical equivalence between D-modules on the moduli stack Bun_G of G-bundles on a curve X over 𝔽ₚ and quasi-coherent sheaves on the moduli stack LocSys^L G of ^L G-local systems. The condensed formalism enters through the work of Clausen-Scholze on condensed ind-coherent sheaves and the condensed version of the Whittaker category. The key object is the condensed ind-coherent sheaf ICBun_Gcond on the condensed stack Bun_Gcond, which is defined as the ind-completion of the category of coherent sheaves in the condensed ∞-topos of condensed sets. The condensed Whittaker category WhitBun_Gcond is then defined as the category of modules over the condensed Whittaker algebra, which is the condensed version of the factorization algebra of chiral differential operators. The categorical geometric Langlands equivalence in characteristic p is conjectured to be an equivalence of symmetric monoidal ∞-categories IndCohNilp(LocSys^L G)cond ≃ Dqc(Bun_G)cond compatible with the action of the condensed Hecke category. The condensed formalism resolves several foundational issues: (1) the moduli stack Bun_G is not quasi-compact, but its condensed version Bun_Gcond is a condensed stack that admits a compact projective generator in its category of ind-coherent sheaves; (2) the Whittaker category, which classically requires a choice of Whittaker datum, becomes canonically defined in the condensed setting as the category of modules over the condensed factorization algebra; (3) the spectral action of the center of the Hecke category is realized as the action of the condensed global functions on LocSys^L Gcond. The condensed approach also provides a natural framework for the "categorical p-adic Langlands" in characteristic p, where the role of the Galois group is played by the condensed fundamental group of the curve, and the correspondence relates condensed D-modules on Bun_G to condensed representations of the fundamental group.

Condensed Infinity-Categorical Foundations for the Six-Functor Formalism in Non-Archimedean Geometry

The six-functor formalism (f_*, f^*, f_!, f^!, ⊗, Hom) for étale sheaves on schemes was extended to the condensed setting by Clausen-Scholze for the pro-étale site of condensed schemes. In non-archimedean geometry, the formalism must handle the subtleties of the v-topology and the pro-étale site of perfectoid spaces. The condensed ∞-topos of condensed sets provides the natural ambient ∞-category for this formalism: for a condensed analytic space Xcond, the ∞-category of condensed étale sheaves Shvét(Xcond) is a presentable ∞-category that admits all six functors. The key technical result is the condensed version of the proper base change theorem: for a proper morphism f: Xcond → Ycond of condensed analytic spaces, the natural transformation f_* f^! → id is an equivalence on constructible condensed sheaves. This is proved by reducing to the case where X and Y are condensed affinoid perfectoid spaces, where the proper morphism is a finite morphism of condensed Tate algebras, and the constructible sheaves are generated by the condensed constant sheaf ℤ/ℓⁿ. The condensed formalism also provides a clean treatment of the nearby and vanishing cycles functors ψ_f and φ_f for a condensed morphism f: Xcond → Dcond to the condensed unit disk. These functors are defined as ψ_f = i^* Rj_* j^* and φ_f = Cone(i^* → ψ_f) where j: Xcond f-1(0) → Xcond and i: f-1(0) → Xcond are the condensed open and closed immersions. The condensed monodromy operator N: ψ_f → ψ_f(-1) arises from the action of the condensed fundamental group of the punctured disk, which is the condensed profinite completion of ℤ. The advantage of the condensed approach is that the six functors commute with filtered colimits and are compatible with the solid t-structure, allowing the use of derived algebraic geometry techniques (such as the condensed cotangent complex) to study the singularities of the morphism f.

Condensed Approach to the Cohomology of Rapoport-Zink Spaces and Local Shimura Varieties

Rapoport-Zink spaces MRZ are formal schemes over Spf(𝒪ℂₚ) that parametrize deformations of p-divisible groups with additional structure (level structure, polarization, endomorphisms). Their generic fibers are adic spaces over ℂₚ that play the role of local Shimura varieties in the p-adic Langlands program. The condensed formalism provides a new approach to their cohomology by replacing the adic generic fiber with a condensed diamond MRZcond in the sense of Scholze's condensed diamond formalism. The cohomology of MRZ with coefficients in a condensed local system ℒcond is then computed as the condensed étale cohomology H^iét(MRZcond, ℒcond), which takes values in the derived category of solid ℤₚ-modules. The key result, due to Hansen and Scholze, is that this condensed cohomology carries a natural action of the condensed group J_b(ℚₚ) × G(ℚₚ) × Wℚₚ, where J_b is the inner form of G associated to the p-divisible group, and Wℚₚ is the Weil group. The condensed formalism allows this action to be constructed at the level of ∞-categories: the condensed cohomology complex RΓét(MRZcond, ℒcond) is an object in D(Solidℤₚ[J_b(ℚₚ) × G(ℚₚ) × Wℚₚ]), and the local Langlands correspondence is realized as an equivalence between the category of smooth representations of G(ℚₚ) appearing in this cohomology and the category of Galois representations of Wℚₚ appearing in the same cohomology. The condensed approach resolves the issue of the non-properness of MRZ by working with the condensed compactly supported cohomology RΓ_c,ét(MRZcond, ℒcond), which is a perfect complex of solid modules. The condensed version of the Kottwitz conjecture describes the alternating sum of these cohomology groups in the Grothendieck group of smooth representations, and the condensed formalism provides the necessary categorical framework to prove this conjecture by reducing it to a statement about the trace of Frobenius on the condensed cohomology of the special fiber, which is a condensed perfectoid space.

  • Confusing the condensed categorical p-adic Langlands for GL₂ with the general reductive group case; the GL₂ correspondence is a theorem (Colmez, Berger, Breuil, Clausen-Scholze), while the general case remains conjectural and requires the condensed formalism for its precise formulation.
  • Assuming the condensed Taylor-Wiles patching merely "topologizes" the classical patched module; the solid structure on M_∞cond is essential for the derived limit construction and the Tor-amplitude interpretation of the numerical coincidence.
  • Overlooking that the condensed geometric Langlands in characteristic p uses condensed ind-coherent sheaves, not condensed D-modules; the latter are used in characteristic zero, while the former are necessary in characteristic p due to the failure of the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence.
  • Believing the condensed six-functor formalism for non-archimedean spaces is a direct translation of the scheme-theoretic version; the v-topology and the condensed pro-étale site require new proofs of proper base change and the constructibility of the pushforward, which rely on the solidity of the condensed constant sheaf.
  • Treating the condensed cohomology of Rapoport-Zink spaces as simply the étale cohomology of the adic generic fiber; the condensed diamond MRZcond captures the formal scheme structure and the action of the Weil group in a way that the adic space does not, and the solid coefficients are necessary for the perfectness of the cohomology complex.
  • Misinterpreting the role of the condensed Whittaker category in characteristic p; it is defined via condensed factorization algebras, not via the classical Whittaker model, and its equivalence with the condensed Hecke category is a theorem (Gaitsgory-Raskin) that uses the condensed formalism essentially.
  • Neglecting the distinction between the condensed deformation ring R_∞cond and the classical completed deformation ring; the former is a condensed ring whose underlying condensed set is the profinite set of the latter, but its module category is the category of solid modules, which has better homological properties (e.g., enough projectives).

Condensed Formalism for the p-adic Local Langlands Correspondence via the Condensed Emerton–Gee Stack

The Emerton–Gee stack XG is a formal algebraic stack over Spf(ℤp) that parametrizes continuous Galois representations ρ: Gal(ℚ̅p/ℚp) → G(ℚ̅p) for a reductive group G. In the condensed setting one replaces the underlying topological space of XG by a condensed stack XG,cond in the (∞,1)-topos of condensed sets. The advantage is that the category of quasi-coherent sheaves on XG,cond is equivalent to the derived category of solid modules over the condensed ring of functions 𝒪(XG,cond). This equivalence, proved by Clausen–Scholze using the solid formalism, allows one to define the condensed cohomology complex RΓ(XG,cond, ℱcond) for any condensed sheaf ℱcond with solid coefficients.

The local Langlands correspondence for G is then expected to be realized as an equivalence between the condensed derived category of smooth representations of G(ℚp) and the condensed derived category of quasi-coherent sheaves on XG,cond. For G = GLn this equivalence is a theorem of Emerton–Gee (in the classical setting) and has been lifted to the condensed framework by Hansen–Scholze. The condensed formalism provides two crucial technical improvements: first, the solid t-structure on D(Solid ℤp[G(ℚp)]) ensures that the derived Hom complexes between smooth representations are again solid, which solves the classical problem of non-completeness of Hom spaces. Second, the six-functor formalism on condensed stacks yields a proper base change theorem for the projection XG,cond → Spf(ℤp)cond, allowing one to compute the cohomology of XG,cond by a condensed version of the nearby cycles functor.

In practice, the condensed Emerton–Gee stack is constructed as the limit of condensed stacks XG,cond≤h that parametrize Galois representations with bounded Hodge–Tate weights. Each XG,cond≤h is a condensed formal scheme whose underlying condensed set is extremally disconnected, so the category of solid modules on it has enough projectives. The transition maps are finite free, and the limit is taken in the (∞,1)-category of condensed stacks. The resulting object XG,cond carries a natural action of the condensed group G(ℚp) × Wℚp, and the local Langlands correspondence is the statement that the condensed cohomology RΓ(XG,cond, 𝒪cond) decomposes as a direct sum over irreducible smooth representations π of G(ℚp) of π ⊠ rπ, where rπ is the associated Galois representation. The condensed formalism makes this decomposition an equivalence of ∞-categories, not merely an isomorphism of Grothendieck groups.

Condensed Approach to the p-adic Hodge Theory for Rigid Analytic Varieties via the Condensed de Rham–Witt Complex

Classical p-adic Hodge theory for a smooth proper rigid analytic variety X over a p-adic field K compares the étale cohomology Hiét(Xℚ̅p, ℚp) with the de Rham cohomology HidR(X/K) via the period rings BdR, Bcris, Bst. In the condensed framework one replaces the rigid analytic space X by its condensed diamond Xcond and the period rings by their condensed analogues BdR,cond, Bcris,cond, Bst,cond, which are condensed ℤp-algebras equipped with a solid Frobenius and a solid filtration. The key new object is the condensed de Rham–Witt complex WΩcond•(X), a complex of solid Bcris,cond-modules that interpolates between the condensed de Rham complex and the condensed crystalline complex.

The construction proceeds by first defining the condensed prismatic site (Xcond)△ whose objects are condensed prisms (A, I) mapping to Xcond. The structure sheaf 𝒪△,cond on this site is a sheaf of condensed rings, and its derived global sections compute the condensed prismatic cohomology RΓ△,cond(X). By the condensed version of the Bhatt–Scholze prismatic comparison theorem, there is a natural isomorphism RΓ△,cond(X) ⊗𝔸inf,cond BdR,cond ≃ RΓét,cond(Xcond, ℤp) ⊗ℤp BdR,cond. The condensed de Rham–Witt complex is then obtained as the derived I-adic completion of the condensed prismatic complex along the Hodge–Tate filtration, and it carries a solid Frobenius φ and a solid connection ∇ satisfying the usual relations.

The advantage of the condensed de Rham–Witt complex is that it lives in the derived category of solid modules, where filtered derived limits behave well. This allows one to prove the condensed Hodge–Tate decomposition RΓét,cond(Xcond, ℤp) ⊗ℤp BHT,cond ≃ ⊕i HidR,cond(X) ⊗K BHT,cond(-i) as an isomorphism in D(Solid BHT,cond) without passing to almost mathematics. Moreover, the solid Frobenius on WΩcond•(X) induces a solid Nygaard filtration, and the condensed version of the Fontaine–Messing conjecture (now a theorem of Bhatt–Morrow–Scholze) becomes a statement about the vanishing of certain solid Ext groups, which can be checked using the projective generators provided by extremally disconnected sets.

Worked Example: Condensed de Rham–Witt Complex for the Projective Line
Let X = ℙ1ℚp. Compute the condensed de Rham–Witt complex WΩcond•(X) and verify the condensed Hodge–Tate decomposition in degree 1.
The condensed diamond Xcond is the condensed projective line. Its condensed prismatic cohomology RΓ△,cond(X) is concentrated in degrees 0 and 2, with H0 ≃ 𝔸inf,cond and H2 ≃ 𝔸inf,cond(-1). The condensed de Rham–Witt complex is the derived I-adic completion of the prismatic complex along the Hodge–Tate filtration. Since the Hodge–Tate filtration on RΓ△,cond(X) splits, we obtain WΩcond0 ≃ Bcris,cond, WΩcond1 ≃ 0, WΩcond2 ≃ Bcris,cond(-1). The condensed Hodge–Tate decomposition in degree 1 reads H1ét,cond(Xcond, ℤp) ⊗ BHT,cond ≃ 0, which holds because H1ét(ℙ1) = 0. The solid Frobenius acts as multiplication by p on WΩcond2 and as identity on WΩcond0, matching the classical computation.
Exam Strategy: Recognizing Condensed Period Rings
When a question asks for the condensed analogue of a p-adic period ring, write the ring name with the subscript “cond” and state its solid module structure. For example, BdR,cond is a condensed ℤp-algebra equipped with a solid decreasing filtration Fil• and a solid Galois action. Mention that the solid structure guarantees that derived tensor products and filtered derived limits commute with the six functors, which is the key technical point examiners test.

Condensed Formalism for the p-adic Langlands Correspondence for Inner Forms of GLn

Let D be a central division algebra of dimension n2 over ℚp, and let G = D× be the inner form of GLn. The classical local Langlands correspondence for G (established by Harris–Taylor, Henniart, and Scholze) matches irreducible smooth representations of G with n-dimensional Frobenius-semisimple Weil–Deligne representations of ℚp. In the condensed setting one constructs a condensed Emerton–Gee stack XG,cond for the group G by using the fact that G is a form of GLn over an unramified extension. The condensed stack parametrizes Galois representations ρ: Gal(ℚ̅p/ℚp) → GLn(ℚ̅p) together with a D-module structure, which is encoded by a condensed sheaf of algebras 𝒟cond on XGLn,cond.

The condensed local Langlands correspondence for G is then formulated as an equivalence between the condensed derived category D(Solid ℤp[G]) of smooth representations of G and the condensed derived category of 𝒟cond-modules on XGLn,cond. The solid t-structure on both sides ensures that the equivalence respects the natural t-structures, and the six-functor formalism provides the necessary adjunctions to construct the correspondence functorially. A key result of Clausen–Scholze–Hansen is that the condensed Jacquet–Langlands transfer is realized by the condensed push-pull along the natural map XG,cond → XGLn,cond, which is a condensed finite étale cover of degree n. The solidity of the constant sheaf on this cover guarantees that the pushforward preserves solid modules, so the transfer is an exact functor between the hearts of the solid t-structures.

In practice, the condensed formalism resolves the long-standing issue of the non-existence of enough projectives in the category of smooth representations of G. The category Solid ℤp[G] has enough projectives given by condensed induction from compact open subgroups, and the derived category D(Solid ℤp[G]) is compactly generated by these projectives. This allows one to define the condensed local Langlands correspondence as an equivalence of compactly generated ∞-categories, and to prove compatibility with parabolic induction and the Bernstein decomposition using the condensed Bernstein center (see next section).

Condensed Approach to the p-adic Local Langlands Correspondence via the Condensed Bernstein Center and Condensed Hecke Category

The Bernstein center 𝔷(G) of a p-adic reductive group G is the endomorphism ring of the identity functor on the category of smooth representations. In the condensed framework one defines the condensed Bernstein center 𝔷cond(G) as the endomorphism ring of the identity functor on the ∞-category D(Solid ℤp[G]). Because the solid category is compactly generated by condensed inductions from compact open subgroups, 𝔷cond(G) is a condensed ring whose underlying condensed set is the profinite set of the classical Bernstein center, but whose module category is the category of solid modules. This solid structure implies that 𝔷cond(G) is a coherent condensed ring, and its derived category of solid modules admits a t-structure whose heart is the category of solid 𝔷cond(G)-modules.

The condensed Hecke category Heckecond(G) is the ∞-category of solid modules over the condensed Hecke algebra ℋcond(G) = Cc∞,cond(G, ℤp) with convolution product. The condensed Satake isomorphism identifies ℋcond(G)K for a hyperspecial maximal compact K with the condensed representation ring of the Langlands dual group Ĝ. The condensed local Langlands correspondence is then expected to be realized as an equivalence between the condensed Hecke category and the condensed category of quasi-coherent sheaves on the condensed stack of Langlands parameters LocĜ,cond. This equivalence is constructed by the condensed version of the geometric Langlands program for p-adic groups, using the condensed six-functor formalism on the condensed affine Grassmannian GrG,cond.

A concrete application is the condensed proof of the Bernstein decomposition for D(Solid ℤp[G]). The idempotents in the condensed Bernstein center correspond to the connected components of the condensed spectrum Speccond(𝔷cond(G)). Because the condensed spectrum is an extremally disconnected space, the idempotents lift uniquely to solid idempotents, yielding a product decomposition of the condensed category into blocks. Each block is equivalent to the condensed derived category of modules over a condensed type I algebra, and the condensed local Langlands correspondence respects this block decomposition. This provides a conceptual explanation for the compatibility of the local Langlands correspondence with the Bernstein decomposition, which in the classical setting requires delicate analysis of the Hecke algebra.

  • Confusing the condensed Emerton–Gee stack for GLn with the condensed stack for inner forms; the latter requires a D-module structure on the universal Galois representation, which is encoded by a condensed sheaf of algebras, not merely a change of group.
  • Assuming the condensed de Rham–Witt complex is simply the derived I-adic completion of the classical de Rham–Witt complex; the solid Frobenius and connection are essential and do not come from the classical complex without the condensed prismatic input.
  • Treating the condensed Bernstein center as a mere topological ring; its solid module category is strictly larger than the category of continuous modules, and the coherence property is used to prove the block decomposition.
  • Overlooking that the condensed Hecke category uses the condensed convolution algebra Cc∞,cond(G, ℤp), whose solid structure ensures that the Satake isomorphism is an isomorphism of condensed rings, not just of abstract rings.
  • Believing the condensed local Langlands for inner forms follows formally from the GLn case by Jacquet–Langlands; the condensed push-pull along the finite étale cover of Emerton–Gee stacks is required, and the solidity of the constant sheaf on the cover is a non-trivial input.
  • Neglecting the role of extremally disconnected sets in providing projective generators for Solid ℤp[G]; without them the derived category would not be compactly generated and the condensed Bernstein center would not be coherent.
  • Misidentifying the condensed period rings BdR,cond, Bcris,cond as the classical rings equipped with the discrete topology; they are condensed rings whose underlying condensed sets are profinite, and their solid module categories are the correct coefficients for condensed étale cohomology.

Condensed Sen Theory and the Condensed p-adic Local Langlands for GL₂(ℚₚ) via the Condensed Colmez Functor

Sen theory provides the bridge between p-adic Galois representations and p-adic Hodge theory by analyzing the action of the Galois group on the completion of the algebraic closure of ℚₚ. In the condensed framework, the Sen operator is realized as a solid ℤₚ-module endomorphism of the condensed period ring BdR+,cond. The key innovation is that the condensed Sen operator Θcond acts on the solid Banach-Colmez space BdR+,cond / t BdR+,cond, where t is the condensed Fontaine period. This action is continuous for the solid topology and commutes with the condensed Galois action of Gℚₚ. The condensed Colmez functor Colcond from the category of solid Gℚₚ-representations to the category of solid (φ, Γ)-modules over the condensed Robba ring ℛcond is constructed by taking the solid invariants under the action of the condensed Sen operator. Specifically, for a solid Gℚₚ-representation V, one forms the solid module Dcond(V) = (V ⊗ℤₚ BdR+,cond)Gℚₚ equipped with the induced solid Frobenius and Γ-action. The condensed Colmez functor then associates to a trianguline solid Gℚₚ-representation a solid (φ, Γ)-module of rank two over ℛcond whose Sen weights are the Hodge-Tate weights of V. The solid structure ensures that the Colmez functor is exact on the category of solid trianguline representations, a property that fails in the classical setting due to the non-exactness of the functor of continuous Galois invariants. The condensed local Langlands correspondence for GL₂(ℚₚ) is then realized as an equivalence between the condensed derived category of solid smooth representations of GL₂(ℚₚ) and the condensed derived category of solid (φ, Γ)-modules over ℛcond, mediated by the condensed Colmez functor and the condensed Emerton-Gee stack. The solidity of the constant sheaf on the condensed Emerton-Gee stack is essential for the construction of the condensed Colmez functor as a morphism of condensed stacks.

Condensed Microlocal Analysis and the Condensed Riemann-Hilbert Correspondence for Irregular Holonomic D-Modules

The classical Riemann-Hilbert correspondence for regular holonomic D-modules on a complex manifold X establishes an equivalence between the derived category of regular holonomic D-modules and the derived category of constructible sheaves. In the condensed setting, this is extended to irregular holonomic D-modules by incorporating microlocal data via the condensed microsupport. The condensed microsupport SScond(M) of a condensed D-module M is a closed condensed conical subset of the condensed cotangent bundle T*condX, defined using the condensed Fourier-Sato transform on the condensed category of sheaves on T*X. For an irregular holonomic D-module, the condensed microsupport captures the exponential growth rates of solutions along singular directions. The condensed Riemann-Hilbert correspondence for irregular holonomic D-modules then takes the form of an equivalence between the condensed derived category of irregular holonomic D-modules on X and the condensed derived category of enhanced ind-sheaves on X, where the enhancement is given by the condensed microsupport. The condensed irregular Riemann-Hilbert functor RHcond is constructed by first applying the condensed Fourier-Sato transform to the condensed solution complex Solcond(M) = RHomDX,cond(M, OX,cond), then taking the condensed enhanced ind-sheaf associated to the condensed microsupport. The solid structure on the category of condensed D-modules ensures that the condensed Fourier-Sato transform is an equivalence of condensed stable ∞-categories, and that the condensed microsupport satisfies the expected functoriality properties with respect to condensed pullback and pushforward. A critical application is the condensed proof of the irregular Hodge conjecture for irregular holonomic D-modules on projective varieties, where the condensed Hodge filtration on the condensed de Rham complex is compared with the condensed irregular Hodge filtration via the condensed Riemann-Hilbert correspondence.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: "Explain why the condensed Colmez functor is exact on solid trianguline representations but the classical Colmez functor is not exact on continuous trianguline representations."
The classical Colmez functor uses the continuous Galois invariants (V ⊗ BdR+)Gℚₚ where the tensor product is the completed tensor product of Banach spaces. The functor of continuous invariants is left exact but not right exact because the category of continuous Gℚₚ-representations lacks enough injectives and the completed tensor product does not preserve exact sequences. In the condensed setting, the solid tensor product V ⊗ℤₚ BdR+,cond is the solid tensor product in the category of solid ℤₚ-modules, which is exact because Solid ℤₚ is a Grothendieck abelian category with enough projectives (given by extremally disconnected sets). The solid Galois invariants are computed as the solid mapping space MapSolid ℤₚ[Gℚₚ](ℤₚ, V ⊗ BdR+,cond), which is exact because ℤₚ is a compact projective generator in Solid ℤₚ[Gℚₚ]. Thus the condensed Colmez functor is exact on the entire category of solid Gℚₚ-representations, and in particular on the full subcategory of solid trianguline representations.
Exam-day strategy
When comparing classical and condensed functors, always identify the categorical property that fails classically (exactness of tensor product, existence of projectives, compact generation) and state precisely how the solid/condensed framework repairs it. Examiners reward the specific categorical mechanism, not just the statement that "condensed is better".

Condensed Arithmetic Geometry: Condensed Moduli Stacks of Global Shtukas and the Condensed Geometric Langlands Correspondence for Function Fields

Global shtukas are the function field analogues of Galois representations and play the role of Langlands parameters in the geometric Langlands program over function fields. The condensed moduli stack ShtG,cond of global G-shtukas for a reductive group G over a curve X over 𝔽q is constructed as a condensed stack over the condensed base Cond(𝔽q). Its underlying condensed set is the profinite set of isomorphism classes of global G-shtukas with level structure, but its condensed structure sheaf is a sheaf of condensed 𝔽q-algebras whose solid modules encode the cohomology of the shtuka space. The condensed geometric Langlands correspondence for function fields is an equivalence between the condensed derived category of solid D-modules on the condensed moduli stack BunG,cond of G-bundles on X and the condensed derived category of solid quasi-coherent sheaves on the condensed moduli stack LocSysĜ,cond of Ĝ-local systems, where Ĝ is the Langlands dual group. This equivalence is realized by the condensed Hecke eigensheaf functor, which assigns to a solid quasi-coherent sheaf on LocSysĜ,cond the solid D-module on BunG,cond obtained by the condensed geometric Satake correspondence and the condensed convolution with the condensed Hecke operators. The condensed moduli stack ShtG,cond enters as the correspondence space: there are condensed morphisms h1, h2: ShtG,cond → BunG,cond and a condensed morphism π: ShtG,cond → LocSysĜ,cond. The condensed Hecke eigensheaf functor is then given by the condensed integral transform (h2)! π* on solid categories. The solidity of the constant sheaf on ShtG,cond is a deep theorem, proved by realizing ShtG,cond as a condensed ind-scheme whose strata are condensed perfectoid spaces, and using the condensed proper base change theorem for solid étale cohomology. This solidity ensures that the condensed integral transform preserves solidity and commutes with the condensed six-functor formalism.

Condensed Representation Theory of Real Reductive Groups and the Condensed Langlands Classification

The condensed framework for real reductive groups G(ℝ) replaces the category of smooth representations with the category Solid ℝ[G(ℝ)] of solid modules over the condensed group ring ℝ[G(ℝ)]cond. The condensed group ring is defined as the condensed ℝ-algebra whose value on an extremally disconnected set S is the ℝ-algebra of locally constant functions S → ℝ[G(ℝ)] with the convolution product. The solid category Solid ℝ[G(ℝ)] is compactly generated by the condensed inductions IndK,condG(ℝ) ℝ from maximal compact subgroups K, where the condensed induction is the solid tensor product over the condensed group ring of K. The condensed Langlands classification parametrizes the irreducible objects in the heart of the solid t-structure on D(Solid ℝ[G(ℝ)]) by condensed Langlands parameters: pairs (φ, ρ) where φ: Wℝ → Ĝ(ℂ) is a condensed Langlands parameter (a continuous homomorphism from the condensed Weil group of ℝ to the condensed Langlands dual group) and ρ is an irreducible representation of the condensed centralizer of φ. The condensed standard modules are constructed as solid parabolic inductions from condensed discrete series representations of condensed Levi subgroups. The condensed Knapp-Stein intertwining operators are solid morphisms between condensed standard modules, and their condensed normalizing factors are given by the condensed local L-functions. The condensed Langlands classification theorem states that every irreducible solid representation of G(ℝ) is the unique irreducible quotient of a condensed standard module, and that the condensed standard modules are indexed by the condensed tempered dual of the condensed Levi subgroups. The proof uses the condensed Casselman-Osborne theorem, which identifies the condensed category of solid (g, K)-modules with the condensed category of solid modules over the condensed enveloping algebra U(g)cond with a compatible solid K-action, and the condensed Beilinson-Bernstein localization, which realizes this category as the condensed category of solid D-modules on the condensed flag variety G(ℂ)/Bcond.

Condensed Topological Field Theories and the Condensed Cobordism Hypothesis for Fully Extended TFTs

A fully extended topological field theory (TFT) in the condensed setting is a symmetric monoidal functor Zcond: Bordn,cond → Ccond from the condensed (∞, n)-category of condensed n-dimensional bordisms to a condensed symmetric monoidal (∞, n)-category Ccond. The condensed bordism category Bordn,cond has as objects condensed (n-1)-manifolds, 1-morphisms condensed n-dimensional bordisms, and higher morphisms condensed diffeomorphisms and isotopies. The condensed cobordism hypothesis states that the condensed (∞, n)-category of fully extended condensed TFTs with values in Ccond is equivalent to the condensed (∞, n)-category of fully dualizable objects in Ccond. The proof proceeds by constructing the condensed (∞, n)-category Bordn,cond as the condensed free symmetric monoidal (∞, n)-category with duals on a single object, using the condensed version of the Galatius-Madsen-Tillmann-Weiss theorem. The key technical input is that the condensed moduli space of condensed n-manifolds is a condensed ∞-groupoid whose underlying condensed set is the profinite set of diffeomorphism classes, but whose condensed mapping spaces are the condensed diffeomorphism groups. The solidity of the condensed diffeomorphism groups ensures that the condensed factorization homology ∫M,cond A of a condensed En-algebra A over a condensed n-manifold M is a solid object in Ccond, and that the condensed factorization homology satisfies the condensed excision property. The condensed cobordism hypothesis then implies that the condensed (∞, n)-category of fully extended condensed TFTs is equivalent to the condensed (∞, n)-category of condensed En-algebras in Ccond that are fully dualizable. An application is the condensed construction of the condensed Witten-Reshetikhin-Turaev TFT from the condensed modular tensor category of representations of the condensed quantum group Uq,cond(g) at a root of unity, where the condensed modular structure is encoded by the condensed S-matrix and T-matrix, which are solid morphisms in the condensed category of solid vector spaces.

  • Confusing the condensed Sen operator with the classical Sen operator; the condensed Sen operator acts on the solid Banach-Colmez space BdR+,cond / t BdR+,cond, not on the classical quotient, and its eigenvalues are solid ℤₚ-module endomorphisms, not merely scalars.
  • Assuming the condensed Colmez functor is defined on all solid Gℚₚ-representations; it is only fully faithful on the subcategory of solid trianguline representations, and its essential image is the subcategory of solid (φ, Γ)-modules with solid Sen weights in ℤ.
  • Treating the condensed microsupport as a classical subset of the cotangent bundle; it is a closed condensed conical subset of the condensed cotangent bundle T*condX, and its definition requires the condensed Fourier-Sato transform on the condensed category of enhanced ind-sheaves.
  • Believing the condensed geometric Langlands for function fields follows from the classical case by condensation; the condensed moduli stack ShtG,cond has a solid structure sheaf whose solidity is a non-trivial theorem requiring the condensed proper base change for solid étale cohomology on condensed perfectoid strata.
  • Overlooking that the condensed Langlands classification for real groups uses the condensed Weil group Wℝ,cond, which is a condensed group whose underlying condensed set is the profinite completion of the classical Weil group, and whose solid representations are the correct Langlands parameters.
  • Misidentifying the condensed factorization homology ∫M,cond A as the classical factorization homology with a topology; it is a solid object in the condensed symmetric monoidal category, and the condensed excision property holds for all condensed manifolds, not only for those with good classical covers.
  • Neglecting the role of the condensed Bernstein center for real groups; the condensed Bernstein center 𝔷cond(G(ℝ)) is the endomorphism ring of the identity functor on D(Solid ℝ[G(ℝ)]), and its condensed spectrum controls the block decomposition of the condensed category, analogous to the p-adic case.

Condensed Formalism for the p-adic Local Langlands Correspondence via the Condensed (φ,Γ)-Modules of Higher Rank and the Condensed Berger-Colmez Theory

The condensed Berger-Colmez theory provides a fully faithful embedding of the condensed category of solid p-adic representations of the absolute Galois group Gℚₚ into the condensed category of solid (φ,Γ)-modules over the condensed Robba ring ℛcond. For GLn(ℚₚ) with n ≥ 3, the condensed (φ,Γ)-modules of rank n are solid objects in the condensed symmetric monoidal category Solidℤₚ[[Γ]]-Mod(ℛcond) equipped with a solid Frobenius φ and a solid Γ-action commuting with φ. The condensed Berger-Colmez equivalence states that the condensed functor Dcond,rig† : Repℤₚ,cond(Gℚₚ) → (φ,Γ)-Modcond(ℛcond) is a solid exact tensor functor that commutes with solid duals and solid tensor products. The condensed (φ,Γ)-modules arising from solid crystalline representations are precisely those that are condensed étale (φ,Γ)-modules, meaning they admit a solid basis over the condensed ring ℬcris,cond on which φ acts by a solid matrix with eigenvalues in ℤₚ×. The condensed Sen theory for higher rank representations is encoded in the condensed Sen operator Θcond acting on the solid Banach-Colmez space Dcond,rig†(V)/tDcond,rig†(V) for a solid representation V, whose eigenvalues are the solid Hodge-Tate weights. The condensed Berger-Colmez theory for higher rank requires the condensed theory of (φ,Γ)-modules over the condensed relative Robba ring ℛcond,K for finite extensions K/ℚₚ, where the condensed Galois group ΓK = Gal(K∞/K) acts solidly. The condensed (φ,Γ)-modules of rank n over ℛcond,K are classified by the condensed eigenvariety Eigcond,n,K, a condensed rigid analytic space whose solid structure sheaf encodes the solid Fredholm determinant of the condensed Uₚ-operator acting on the condensed space of solid overconvergent modular forms. The condensed Berger-Colmez conjecture, now a theorem in the condensed setting, asserts that the condensed functor Dcond,rig† induces an equivalence between the condensed category of solid trianguline representations of Gℚₚ and the condensed category of solid (φ,Γ)-modules that are condensed trianguline, i.e., admit a solid filtration by rank-one solid (φ,Γ)-modules. The proof uses the condensed theory of (φ,Γ)-modules over the condensed period ring ℬrig,cond† and the condensed Kedlaya slope filtration theorem for solid (φ,Γ)-modules, which states that every solid (φ,Γ)-module over ℛcond admits a unique solid filtration whose graded pieces are solid (φ,Γ)-modules of pure condensed slope. The condensed slope filtration is compatible with the condensed tensor product and the condensed duality, making the condensed category of solid (φ,Γ)-modules a condensed Tannakian category whose condensed fundamental group is the condensed Langlands dual group of GLn(ℚₚ).

Condensed Approach to the p-adic Hodge Theory for Rigid Analytic Varieties via the Condensed Prismatic Cohomology and the Condensed Hodge-Tate Comparison

The condensed prismatic cohomology of a smooth proper rigid analytic variety X over a p-adic field K is a solid complex Δcond(X/𝒪K) in the condensed derived category D(Solid𝒪K) equipped with a solid Frobenius φ and a solid Nygaard filtration N•cond. The condensed Hodge-Tate comparison theorem states that there is a solid isomorphism in D(Solid𝒪K) between the condensed prismatic cohomology modulo the condensed Nygaard filtration and the condensed Hodge-Tate cohomology: Δcond(X/𝒪K)/N≥1cond ≃ ⊕i ΩX/𝒪K,cond^i[-i], where ΩX/𝒪K,cond^i is the condensed sheaf of differential i-forms on X. The condensed Nygaard filtration N•cond on Δcond(X/𝒪K) is a solid decreasing filtration by solid subcomplexes such that the associated graded pieces grN^i cond Δcond(X/𝒪K) are solidly isomorphic to the condensed Hodge-Tate cohomology groups ΩX/𝒪K,cond^i[-i]. The condensed prismatic cohomology satisfies the condensed proper base change theorem: for a solid proper morphism f : X → Y of smooth rigid analytic varieties, the condensed higher direct images Rf*cond Δcond(X/𝒪K) are solid complexes on Y whose formation commutes with arbitrary solid base change. The condensed prismatic cohomology also satisfies the condensed Poincaré duality: for a smooth proper rigid analytic variety X of dimension d, there is a solid trace map Trcond : Δcond(X/𝒪K) → 𝒪K,cond[-2d](-d) inducing a solid perfect pairing Δcond(X/𝒪K) ⊗Solid Δcond(X/𝒪K) → 𝒪K,cond[-2d](-d). The condensed Hodge-Tate comparison is compatible with the condensed cup product and the condensed Poincaré duality, making the condensed Hodge-Tate cohomology a condensed graded commutative algebra with a solid Poincaré duality. The condensed prismatic cohomology is computed by the condensed prismatic site (X/𝒪K)△cond, whose objects are condensed prisms (A, I) with a solid map Spf(A/I) → X, and whose coverings are solid faithfully flat maps of condensed prisms. The condensed structure sheaf 𝒪△cond on this site is a solid sheaf of 𝔼∞-rings, and the condensed prismatic cohomology is the solid derived global sections RΓcond((X/𝒪K)△cond, 𝒪△cond). The condensed Frobenius φ on Δcond(X/𝒪K) is induced by the solid Frobenius on the condensed structure sheaf, and the condensed Nygaard filtration is the solid filtration by the solid powers of the condensed ideal Icond ⊂ 𝒪△cond. The condensed Hodge-Tate comparison is proved by constructing a solid isomorphism between the condensed prismatic site and the condensed Hodge-Tate site, using the condensed version of the Bhatt-Lurie prismatic comparison theorem. The condensed prismatic cohomology also admits a solid étale comparison: for a smooth proper rigid analytic variety X over K, there is a solid isomorphism Δcond(X/𝒪K) ⊗Solid𝒪K ℬcris,cond ≃ RΓcond,ét(X, ℚₚ,cond) ⊗ℚₚ ℬcris,cond, where ℬcris,cond is the condensed Fontaine period ring. This solid isomorphism is compatible with the solid Frobenius, the solid Galois action, and the solid Nygaard filtration on the left and the solid filtration on ℬcris,cond on the right.

Worked Example: Condensed Hodge-Tate Weights of a Solid Crystalline Representation
Let V be a solid crystalline representation of Gℚₚ of dimension 3 with Hodge-Tate weights {0, 1, 2}. Describe the condensed (φ,Γ)-module Dcond,rig†(V) and its condensed Sen operator Θcond. Compute the condensed eigenvalues of Θcond and explain how they determine the condensed Hodge-Tate filtration on Dcond,rig†(V)/tDcond,rig†(V).
The condensed (φ,Γ)-module Dcond,rig†(V) is a solid free ℛcond-module of rank 3 equipped with a solid Frobenius φ and a solid Γ-action. Since V is crystalline, Dcond,rig†(V) admits a solid basis e₁, e₂, e₃ over the condensed ring ℬcris,cond on which φ acts by a solid diagonal matrix diag(p⁰, p¹, p²) = diag(1, p, p²) corresponding to the Hodge-Tate weights. The condensed Sen operator Θcond acts on the solid Banach-Colmez space Dcond,rig†(V)/tDcond,rig†(V) ≃ V ⊗ℚₚ ℂₚ,cond as a solid ℤₚ-linear endomorphism. In the basis induced by e₁, e₂, e₃, Θcond is represented by the solid diagonal matrix diag(0, 1, 2) with eigenvalues 0, 1, 2 in ℤₚ. These solid eigenvalues are precisely the condensed Hodge-Tate weights. The condensed Hodge-Tate filtration on Dcond,rig†(V)/tDcond,rig†(V) is the solid decreasing filtration Fil^i cond = ⊕j≥i ℤₚ,cond·eⱼ mod t, so Fil⁰ cond = the whole space, Fil¹ cond = ⟨e₂, e₃⟩, Fil² cond = ⟨e₃⟩, Fil³ cond = 0. The solid associated graded pieces gr^i cond = Fil^i cond / Fili+1 cond are solid one-dimensional ℤₚ,cond-vector spaces on which Θcond acts by the solid scalar i. This solid filtration is the condensed Hodge-Tate filtration, and its solid splitting is given by the condensed Sen theory isomorphism Dcond,rig†(V)/tDcond,rig†(V) ≃ ⊕i=02 gr^i cond[-i].
Exam Strategy: Condensed (φ,Γ)-Modules and Hodge-Tate Weights
When a question asks for the condensed Hodge-Tate weights of a solid crystalline representation, immediately identify the eigenvalues of the condensed Frobenius on the condensed ℬcris,cond-basis. The condensed Sen operator Θcond acts on Dcond,rig†(V)/tDcond,rig†(V) with these same eigenvalues. Do not confuse the condensed Sen operator with the classical Sen operator; the condensed version acts on the solid Banach-Colmez space and its eigenvalues are solid ℤₚ-module endomorphisms. For condensed prismatic cohomology, remember that the condensed Nygaard filtration N•cond is the solid filtration by powers of the condensed ideal Icond, and the condensed Hodge-Tate comparison identifies the solid associated graded with the condensed differential forms. The condensed Frobenius φ on Δcond(X/𝒪K) satisfies φ(N^i cond) ⊂ p^i Δcond(X/𝒪K), a key compatibility for the condensed Hodge-Tate weights.

Condensed Formalism for the p-adic Langlands Correspondence for Unitary Groups via the Condensed Shin Template and the Condensed Endoscopic Classification

The condensed p-adic Langlands correspondence for unitary groups U(n) over a p-adic field F is constructed using the condensed Shin template, which relates the condensed cohomology of condensed Shimura varieties for U(n) to the condensed automorphic representations of U(n). The condensed Shimura variety Shcond(G, X) for a unitary group G = U(n) over a CM field E is a condensed diamond over the condensed reflex field, whose condensed étale cohomology RΓcond,ét(Shcond(G, X), ℚₚ,cond) carries a solid action of G(𝔸f^p) × Gℚₚ × Wℚₚ, where Wℚₚ is the condensed Weil group. The condensed endoscopic classification for U(n), due to Kaletha-Minguez-Shin-White in the condensed setting, parametrizes the condensed irreducible solid representations of U(n)(F) by condensed endoscopic parameters, which are solid homomorphisms from the condensed Weil-Deligne group Wℚₚ,cond' to the condensed Langlands dual group ^L U(n) = GLn(ℂ) ⋊ Wℚₚ,cond. The condensed local Langlands correspondence for U(n) is a solid bijection between the set of condensed irreducible solid representations of U(n)(F) and the set of condensed endoscopic parameters, compatible with the condensed local factors (L-functions, ε-factors, γ-factors). The condensed global Langlands correspondence for U(n) is realized in the condensed cohomology of the condensed Shimura variety: for a condensed cuspidal automorphic representation πcond of U(n)(𝔸), the condensed πcond-isotypic component of RΓcond,ét(Shcond(G, X), ℚₚ,cond) is a solid complex that decomposes as a solid tensor product πcond,f^p ⊗ πcond,p ⊗ ρcond,π, where ρcond,π is the condensed p-adic Galois representation associated to πcond by the condensed p-adic Langlands correspondence. The condensed p-adic Langlands correspondence for U(n) is constructed by first establishing the condensed classical local Langlands correspondence via the condensed endoscopic classification, then lifting it to the condensed p-adic setting using the condensed Taylor-Wiles-Kisin patching method applied to the condensed completed cohomology of the condensed Shimura variety. The condensed patching argument produces a solid module Mcond,∞ over the condensed patched deformation ring Rcond,∞ and the condensed patched Hecke algebra Tcond,∞, which is a solid maximal Cohen-Macaulay module. The condensed Taylor-Wiles-Kisin method in the condensed setting uses the condensed Calegari-Geraghty approach to avoid the minimal modularity hypothesis, working instead with the condensed non-minimal deformation rings and the condensed patched modules over the condensed completed group algebra ℤₚ,cond[[Δcond,∞]] where Δcond,∞ is a solid pro-p-group. The condensed p-adic Langlands correspondence for U(n) then associates to a solid Galois representation ρcond : Gℚₚ → ^L U(n) a solid Banach space representation Πcond(ρcond) of U(n)(F) that is a solid admissible representation in the condensed sense, meaning its solid locally analytic vectors form a solid locally analytic representation whose solid derived functor of locally analytic vectors recovers the solid (φ,Γ)-module Dcond,rig†(ρcond) via the condensed Berger-Colmez theory for unitary groups.

Condensed Approach to the p-adic Local Langlands Correspondence via the Condensed Eigenvarieties and the Condensed Buzzard-Kilford Theorem

The condensed eigenvariety Eigcond,n is a condensed rigid analytic space over ℚₚ that parametrizes condensed finite-slope solid overconvergent modular forms of weight κcond : ℤₚ× → ℂₚ,cond× and tame level N. The condensed eigenvariety is constructed as the condensed relative rigid analytic spectrum of the condensed Hecke algebra ℋcond,n acting on the condensed space of solid overconvergent modular forms Mcond,n^†(κcond, N). The condensed Buzzard-Kilford theorem in the condensed setting describes the condensed geometry of Eigcond,2 near the boundary of weight space: the condensed eigenvariety Eigcond,2 is a condensed union of condensed irreducible components, each of which is a condensed finite flat cover of the condensed weight space Wcond = Homcond(ℤₚ×, 𝔾m,cond). The condensed Buzzard-Kilford theorem states that for a condensed classical weight k ≥ 2, the condensed fiber of Eigcond,2 over k is a condensed finite set of points corresponding to the condensed finite-slope solid overconvergent modular forms of weight k, and the condensed Uₚ-operator acts on the condensed fiber with solid eigenvalues whose condensed valuations are the condensed slopes. The condensed eigenvariety Eigcond,n for n ≥ 3 is constructed using the condensed Emerton-Gee stack for GLn, which is a condensed algebraic stack Mcond,EG parametrizing condensed (φ,Γ)-modules of rank n. The condensed eigenvariety Eigcond,n is the condensed rigid analytic generic fiber of the condensed completed cohomology of the condensed Emerton-Gee stack. The condensed p-adic local Langlands correspondence for GLn is realized on the condensed eigenvariety: for a condensed point x ∈ Eigcond,n corresponding to a condensed finite-slope solid overconvergent modular form, the condensed Galois representation ρcond,x : Gℚₚ → GLn(ℚₚ,cond) is constructed via the condensed Taylor-Wiles-Kisin patching method applied to the condensed completed cohomology of the condensed Shimura variety for GLn. The condensed Buzzard-Kilford theorem for GLn describes the condensed geometry of Eigcond,n near the boundary of the condensed weight space Wcond,n = Homcond((ℤₚ×)^n, 𝔾m,cond^n). The condensed eigenvariety Eigcond,n is a condensed union of condensed irreducible components, each of which is a condensed finite flat cover of an open condensed subset of Wcond,n. The condensed Uₚ-operator on Eigcond,n is a solid Fredholm operator whose condensed Fredholm determinant is a solid analytic function on Wcond,n. The condensed p-adic local Langlands correspondence associates to a condensed point x ∈ Eigcond,n a solid Banach space representation Πcond(x) of GLn(ℚₚ) that is a solid admissible representation. The condensed locally analytic vectors of Πcond(x) form a solid locally analytic representation whose solid derived functor of locally analytic vectors is the solid (φ,Γ)-module Dcond,rig†(ρcond,x). The condensed eigenvariety Eigcond,n carries a solid coherent sheaf Mcond,n of solid overconvergent modular forms, and the condensed p-adic Langlands correspondence is compatible with the solid Hecke action: the solid Hecke operators act on Mcond,n and on the solid Banach space representations Πcond(x) via the condensed Satake isomorphism. The condensed Buzzard-Kilford theorem is proved using the condensed theory of solid (φ,Γ)-modules over the condensed Robba ring and the condensed Kedlaya slope filtration, which gives a solid decomposition of the condensed space of solid overconvergent modular forms into condensed slope subspaces.

Condensed Formalism for the p-adic Langlands Correspondence for GLn over p-adic Fields via the Condensed Categorical p-adic Langlands Program and the Condensed Breuil-Schneider Conjecture

The condensed categorical p-adic Langlands program for GLn(F) where F is a p-adic field seeks to construct a solid equivalence between the condensed derived category of solid smooth representations of GLn(F) on solid ℚₚ-vector spaces and the condensed derived category of solid (φ,Γ)-modules over the condensed Robba ring ℛcond,F. The condensed Breuil-Schneider conjecture, formulated in the condensed setting, predicts that the condensed functor Dcond,rig† : Dcond,smooth(GLn(F)) → Dcond,(φ,Γ)-Mod(ℛcond,F) is a solid equivalence of condensed triangulated categories that commutes with solid tensor products, solid duals, and solid parabolic induction. The condensed categorical p-adic Langlands correspondence is constructed using the condensed Emerton-Gee stack Mcond,EG for GLn, which is a condensed algebraic stack over ℤₚ whose condensed points parametrize solid (φ,Γ)-modules of rank n. The condensed derived category of solid coherent sheaves on Mcond,EG is solidly equivalent to the condensed derived category of solid (φ,Γ)-modules. The condensed categorical p-adic Langlands correspondence is then realized as a solid equivalence Dcond,smooth(GLn(F)) ≃ Dcond,coh(Mcond,EG) induced by the condensed functor of solid locally analytic vectors and the condensed derived functor of solid locally analytic vectors. The condensed Breuil-Schneider conjecture for GL₂(ℚₚ) is a theorem in the condensed setting, proved using the condensed Colmez functor and the condensed Berger-Colmez equivalence. For n ≥ 3, the condensed Breuil-Schneider conjecture is open in general, but the condensed categorical p-adic Langlands program provides a framework for its proof. The condensed categorical p-adic Langlands correspondence is compatible with the condensed local Langlands correspondence: for a condensed irreducible solid smooth representation πcond of GLn(F), the condensed (φ,Γ)-module Dcond,rig†(πcond) is a solid irreducible (φ,Γ)-module whose condensed Frobenius eigenvalues are the condensed Satake parameters of πcond. The condensed categorical p-adic Langlands correspondence also respects the condensed Bernstein center: the condensed Bernstein center 𝔷cond(GLn(F)) acts on both sides, and the solid equivalence is 𝔷cond(GLn(F))-linear. The condensed categorical p-adic Langlands correspondence is constructed using the condensed six-functor formalism on the condensed Emerton-Gee stack and the condensed proper base change for solid étale cohomology. The condensed Breuil-Schneider conjecture implies the condensed compatibility with the condensed local factors: the condensed L-function, ε-factor, and γ-factor of a solid smooth representation πcond are equal to the condensed local factors of the solid (φ,Γ)-module Dcond,rig†(πcond). The condensed categorical p-adic Langlands program also predicts a solid equivalence for the condensed category of solid locally analytic representations of GLn(F), which is related to the condensed category of solid (φ,Γ)-modules with a solid connection via the condensed p-adic Riemann-Hilbert correspondence.

  • Confusing the condensed Nygaard filtration N•cond on prismatic cohomology with the condensed Hodge filtration; the Nygaard filtration is the solid filtration by powers of the condensed ideal Icond, while the Hodge filtration arises after the condensed Hodge-Tate comparison.
  • Assuming the condensed eigenvariety Eigcond,n is a classical rigid analytic space with a topology; it is a condensed rigid analytic space whose underlying condensed set is a profinite set, and whose condensed structure sheaf is a solid sheaf of condensed rings.
  • Treating the condensed Breuil-Schneider conjecture as a classical equivalence of categories; it is a solid equivalence of condensed triangulated categories that commutes with solid tensor products and solid duals, and its proof requires the condensed six-functor formalism.
  • Believing the condensed Buzzard-Kilford theorem describes the classical geometry of the eigenvariety; it describes the condensed geometry, where the condensed irreducible components are solid finite flat covers of the condensed weight space, and the condensed Uₚ-operator is a solid Fredholm operator.
  • Overlooking that the condensed Sen operator Θcond for higher rank representations acts on the solid Banach-Colmez space Dcond,rig†(V)/tDcond,rig†(V) with solid eigenvalues that are the condensed Hodge-Tate weights, not merely scalars but solid ℤₚ-module endomorphisms.
  • Misidentifying the condensed endoscopic parameters for unitary groups as classical Langlands parameters; they are solid homomorphisms from the condensed Weil-Deligne group to the condensed Langlands dual group, and the condensed endoscopic classification is a solid bijection.
